such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
.
For a given
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, define

{\cal M}_{p,j}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by

{\cal M}_{p,j}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j=0\\
\left\{\delta_{p,j,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.31)

For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
the linear functional

\delta_{p,0,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,0,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(0)}(p)\right)

(cf.
(
5.15
)
).
For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
and
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
, the linear functional

\delta_{p,j,v,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,j,v,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(j)}(p)[v]\right)

(cf.
(
5.16
)
).
For a given
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
define

{\cal T}_{p,l}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by
(cf.
(
5.19
)
)

{\cal T}_{p,l}:=\bigcup_{j=0}^{l}{\cal M}_{p,j}.

(5.32)

Then for every
\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}
and any

\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
we have

|\sigma(\phi)|\leq\Lambda^{l}_{\phi}(p)\leq||\phi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)}.

(5.33)

Consequently, for every
\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}
, we have

||\sigma||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}}\leq 1\qquad\text{so that}\qquad{\cal T}_{p,l}\subset{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}}(0,1).

(5.34)

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Let
c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and assume that
V
and
W
are
real Banach spaces of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively.
Let
{\cal M}\subset V
be closed with
p\in{\cal M}

and assume that the tensor
powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible norms
(cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\in V
be a unit
V
-norm basis of
V
.
For each integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
define
(cf.
(
5.29
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\right\}\subset V^{\otimes n}

(5.35)

and (cf.
(
5.30
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}^{\ord}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\text{ such that }l_{1}\leq\ldots\leq l_{n}\right\}\subset{\cal V}_{n}\subset V^{\otimes n}.

(5.36)

Further, let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{c}\in W
be a unit
W
-norm
basis of
W
, and let

w_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,w_{c}^{\ast}\in W^{\ast}

be the corresponding dual basis of
W^{\ast}
.

Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
.
For a given
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, define

{\cal M}_{p,j}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by
(cf.
(
5.31
)
)

{\cal M}_{p,j}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j=0\\
\left\{\delta_{p,j,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.37)

For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
the linear functional

\delta_{p,0,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,0,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(0)}(p)\right)

(cf.
(
5.15
)
).
For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
and
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
, the linear functional

\delta_{p,j,v,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,j,v,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(j)}(p)[v]\right)

(cf.
(
5.16
)
).
For a given
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
define

{\cal T}_{p,l}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by
(cf.
(
5.32
)
)

{\cal T}_{p,l}:=\bigcup_{j=0}^{l}{\cal M}_{p,j}.

(5.38)

Now fix
p\in{\cal M}
and
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Consider
\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}
.
It follows from
(
5.37
)

and
(
5.38
)
that either

\sigma=\delta_{p,0,s}
for some
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
or
\sigma=\delta_{p,j,v,s}
for some
j\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
,
some
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
, and some
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
.

If
\sigma=\delta_{p,0,s}
for some
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}

then, given any
\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, we may appeal to Lemma

5.2
to deduce that
(cf.
(
5.24
)
)

\left|\sigma(\phi)\right|=\left|\delta_{p,0,s}(\phi)\right)\leq\left|\left|\phi^{(0)}(p)\right|\right|_{W}\leq\Lambda^{l}_{\phi}(p)\leq||\phi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)}.

(5.39)

If
\sigma=\delta_{p,j,v,s}
for some
j\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
,
some
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
, and some
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
then we first note that
||v||_{V^{\otimes j}}=1
.
Thus, given any
\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, we may appeal to Lemma

5.2
to deduce that
(cf.
(
5.25
)
)

\left|\sigma(\phi)\right|=\left|\delta_{p,j,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)[v]\right|\right|_{W}\leq\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq\Lambda^{l}_{\phi}(p)\leq||\phi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)}.

(5.40)

Together,
(
5.39
)

and
(
5.40
)
establish
the claim made in
(
5.33
)
.
Since the
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
norm of
\sigma

is given by

||\sigma||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}}:=\sup\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)\text{ with }||\phi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)}=1\right\}
,
the claims made in
(
5.34
)
are
an immediate consequence of

(
5.33
)
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
5.3
.
∎

We now use the inequalities established in Lemma

5.2
to establish, for
each
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, that the
symmetric
j
-linear form from
V
to
W
given by

\phi^{(j)}(p)\in{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)
is determined,
in a quantified sense, by the set real numbers

\left\{\sigma(\phi):\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
where
{\cal M}_{p,j}
is defined in

(
5.17
)
.
The precise result is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4

(Linear Functionals Determine Pointwise Value)
.

Let
c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and assume that
V
and
W
are
real Banach spaces of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively.
Let
{\cal M}\subset V
be closed with
p\in{\cal M}

and assume that the tensor
powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible norms
(cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\in V
be a unit
V
-norm basis
of
V
.
For each integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
define
(cf.
(
5.3
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\right\}\subset V^{\otimes n}

(5.41)

and (cf.
(
5.4
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}^{\ord}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\text{ such that }l_{1}\leq\ldots\leq l_{n}\right\}\subset{\cal V}_{n}\subset V^{\otimes n}.

(5.42)

Further, let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{c}\in W
be a unit
W
-norm
basis of
W
, and let

w_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,w_{c}^{\ast}\in W^{\ast}

be the corresponding dual basis of
W^{\ast}
.

Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
.
For a given
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, define

{\cal M}_{p,j}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by

{\cal M}_{p,j}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j=0\\
\left\{\delta_{p,j,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.43)

For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
the linear functional

\delta_{p,0,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,0,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(0)}(p)\right)

(cf.
(
5.15
)
).
For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
and
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
, the linear functional

\delta_{p,j,v,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,j,v,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(j)}(p)[v]\right)

(cf.
(
5.16
)
).

Suppose that
A\geq 0
and

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
.
Then for any
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
we have that the
following implications are valid.
Firstly,

\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq A\implies\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}\leq A.

(5.44)

Secondly,

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}\leq A\implies\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq cd^{j}A.

(5.45)

Consequently, in the case that
A=0
, the combination of

(
5.44
)
and
(
5.45
)
yields
that

\phi^{(j)}(p)\equiv 0\text{ in }{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)\qquad\iff\qquad\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}=0.

(5.46)

Proof of Lemma

5.4
.

Let
c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and assume that
V
and
W
are
real Banach spaces of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively.
Let
{\cal M}\subset V
be closed with
p\in{\cal M}

and assume that the tensor
powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible norms
(cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\in V
be a unit
V
-norm basis
of
V
.
For each integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
define
(cf.
(
5.41
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\right\}\subset V^{\otimes n}

(5.47)

and (cf.
(
5.42
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}^{\ord}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\text{ such that }l_{1}\leq\ldots\leq l_{n}\right\}\subset{\cal V}_{n}\subset V^{\otimes n}.

(5.48)

A consequence of the tensor powers of
V
being equipped
with admissible norms in the sense of Definition

2.1
is that

{\cal V}_{n}\subset V^{\otimes}
is a unit
V^{\otimes n}
-norm
basis of
V^{\otimes n}
.
Further, let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{c}\in W
be a unit
W
-norm
basis of
W
, and let

w_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,w_{c}^{\ast}\in W^{\ast}

be the corresponding dual basis of
W^{\ast}
.

Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
.
For a given
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, define

{\cal M}_{p,j}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by
(cf.
(
5.43
)
)

{\cal M}_{p,j}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j=0\\
\left\{\delta_{p,j,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.49)

For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
the linear functional

\delta_{p,0,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,0,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(0)}(p)\right)

(cf.
(
5.15
)
).
For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
and
v\in V^{\otimes j}
, the linear functional

\delta_{p,j,v,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,j,v,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(j)}(p)[v]\right)

(cf.
(
5.16
)
).

Suppose that

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
.
Observe that if
(
5.44
)

and
(
5.45
)
are both valid for any

A\geq 0
, then
the equivalence claimed in
(
5.46
)

is an immediate consequence of invoking both for the
particular choice of
A:=0
.
Thus we need only verify that both the implications

(
5.44
)
and
(
5.45
)

are valid for any
A\geq 0
.
Thus, for the remainder of the proof, we fix an arbitrary
choice of
A\geq 0
.

We begin with the case that
j=0
so that
(cf.
(
5.49
)
)

{\cal M}_{p,0}=\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
.
By appealing to Lemma
5.2

we may conclude that, for each
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
we have (cf.
(
5.24
)
)

\left|\delta_{p,0,s}(\phi)\right|\leq\left|\left|\phi^{(0)}(p)\right|\right|_{W}\leq\sum_{a=1}^{c}\left|\delta_{p,0,s}(\phi)\right|.

(5.50)

If
\left|\left|\phi^{(0)}(p)\right|\right|_{W}\leq A

then, for any
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
, a consequence of

(
5.50
)
is that

|\delta_{p,0,s}(\phi)|\leq A
. Therefore, since

{\cal M}_{p,0}=\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}
,
we deduce that

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,0}\right\}\leq A
.
Thus, recalling that
{\cal L}(V^{\otimes 0};W)=W
and we choose
to equip this space with the norm
||\cdot||_{W}
on
W

(cf. Remark
2.3
),
the implication claimed in
(
5.44
)

is valid when
j=0
.

If

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,0}\right\}\leq A
,
then since

{\cal M}_{p,0}=\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}

we have, for every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
, that

\left|\delta_{p,0,s}(\phi)\right|\leq A
.
Therefore, via an application of
(
5.50
)
,
it follows that
\left|\left|\phi^{(0)}(p)\right|\right|_{W}\leq cA
.
Thus, recalling that
{\cal L}(V^{\otimes 0};W)=W
and we choose
to equip this space with the norm
||\cdot||_{W}
on
W

(cf. Remark
2.3
),
the implication claimed in
(
5.45
)

is valid when
j=0
.

Hence we have established that the lemma is true when
j=0
.

We next establish that the lemma is true when
j=1
.
In this case, since
{\cal V}_{1}^{\ord}={\cal V}_{1}
, we have

{\cal M}_{p,1}=\left\{\delta_{p,1,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}_{1}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
.
Given
v\in{\cal V}_{1}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
we can appeal to Lemma
5.2

to conclude that (cf.
(
5.25
)
)

\left|\delta_{p,1,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq\left|\left|\phi^{(1)}(p)[v]\right|\right|_{W}\leq\sum_{a=1}^{c}\left|\delta_{p,1,v,a}(\phi)\right|.

(5.51)

If
\left|\left|\phi^{(1)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V;W)}\leq A

then, since
v\in{\cal V}_{1}
means
||v||_{V}=1
, it follows from

(
5.51
)
that

\left|\delta_{p,1,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq A
.
The arbitrariness of
v\in{\cal V}_{1}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}

means we can deduce that

\max\left\{\left|\sigma(\phi)\right|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,1}\right\}\leq A
.
Thus the implication
(
5.44
)
is valid
when
j=1
.

If
\max\left\{\left|\sigma(\phi)\right|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,1}\right\}\leq A
then, for every

v\in{\cal V}_{1}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
, we have that

\left|\delta_{p,1,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq A
.
Now suppose that
v\in V
with
||v||_{V}=1
.
Then, since
{\cal V}_{1}
is a basis of
V
, there are coefficients

b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

v=\sum_{m=1}^{d}b_{m}v_{m}
.
Moreover, by appealing to Lemma

5.1
,
we have (cf.
(
5.7
)
for
m=1
) that

\sum_{j=m}^{d}|b_{m}|\leq d
.
Therefore, via an application of
(
5.51
)
,
we have

\left|\left|\phi^{(1)}(p)[v]\right|\right|_{W}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{d}|b_{m}|\left|\left|\phi^{(1)}(p)[v_{m}]\right|\right|_{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:de_1_ests_B}})}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{m=1}^{d}|b_{m}|\sum_{a=1}^{c}\left|\delta_{p,1,v_{m},a}(\phi)\right|\leq\sum_{m=1}^{d}|b_{m}|\sum_{a=1}^{c}A\leq cdA.

(5.52)

By taking the supremum over
v\in V
with
||v||_{V}=1

in
(
5.52
)
, we obtain that

\left|\left|\phi^{(1)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V;W)}\leq cdA
.
Thus the implication
(
5.45
)
is
valid when
j=1
.

Hence we have established that the lemma is true when
j=1
.

We complete the proof by establishing that, when

k\geq 2
, the lemma is
true for an arbitrary fixed
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
.
In this case, we have

{\cal M}_{p,j}=\left\{\delta_{p,j,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}^{\ord}_{j}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
.
Given
v\in{\cal V}^{\ord}_{j}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
we can appeal to Lemma
5.2

to conclude that (cf.
(
5.25
)
)

\left|\delta_{p,j,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)[v]\right|\right|_{W}\leq\sum_{a=1}^{c}\left|\delta_{p,j,v,a}(\phi)\right|.

(5.53)

If
\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq A
,
since
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
means
||v||_{V^{\otimes j}}=1
,
it follows from
(
5.53
)
that

\left|\delta_{p,j,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq A
.
The arbitrariness of both
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
and

s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
means we can deduce that

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}\leq A
.
Thus the implication
(
5.44
)
is valid
for arbitrary
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
.

Now suppose that

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}\leq A
.
We first claim that this means, given any
v\in{\cal V}_{j}
and
any
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
, that

\left|\delta_{p,j,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq A
.
Indeed, if
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\subset{\cal V}_{j}
then this
is an immediate consequence of the definition of
{\cal M}_{p,j}
.
And it follows for general
v\in{\cal V}_{j}
as follows.
Fix
v\in{\cal V}_{j}
and
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
.
Then there exists a permutation
\rho\in S_{j}
such that

\rho(v)\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
; see Definition

2.1
for the details of how the
permutation group
S_{j}
acts on the tensor product
V^{\otimes j}
.
But
\phi^{(j)}(p)\in{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)
is a

j
-symmetric linear form from
V
to
W
; so, in particular,

\phi^{(j)}(p)[\rho(v)]=\phi^{(j)}(p)[v]
.
Hence
\delta_{p,j,v,s}(\phi)=\delta_{p,j,\rho(v),s}(\phi)

from which it is immediate that

\left|\delta_{p,j,v,s}(\phi)\right|\leq A
as claimed.

Let
v\in V^{\otimes j}
with
||v||_{V^{\otimes j}}=1
.
Then, by appealing to Lemma

5.1
,
we have that there are coefficients

\left\{C_{l_{1}\dots l_{j}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{j})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{j}\right\}
for which
(cf.
(
5.6
)
)

v=\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{d}\ldots\sum_{l_{j}=1}^{d}C_{l_{1}\dots l_{j}}v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{j}},

(5.54)

and such that (cf.
(
5.7
)
for
A
there as
1
)

\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{d}\dots\sum_{l_{j}=1}^{d}\left|C_{l_{1}\dots l_{j}}\right|\leq d^{j}.

(5.55)

Consequently, via an application of

(
5.53
)
, we have

\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)[v]\right|\right|_{W}\leq\sum_{l_{1}}^{d}\dots\sum_{l_{j}=1}^{d}\left|C_{l_{1}\dots l_{j}}\right|\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\left[v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{j}}\right]\right|\right|_{W}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:de_j_ests_B}})}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{l_{1}}^{d}\dots\sum_{l_{j}=1}^{d}\left|C_{l_{1}\dots l_{j}}\right|\sum_{a=1}^{c}\left|\delta_{p,j,v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{j}},a}(\phi)\right|\leq cA\sum_{l_{1}}^{d}\dots\sum_{l_{j}=1}^{d}\left|C_{l_{1}\dots l_{j}}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{coeff_sum_bound_level_j}})}}}{{\leq}}cd^{j}A.

(5.56)

By taking the supremum over
v\in V^{\otimes j}
with

||v||_{V^{\otimes j}}=1
in
(
5.56
)
,
we obtain that

\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq cd^{j}A
.
Thus the implication
(
5.45
)
is valid for
arbitrary
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
.
This completes the proof of Lemma

5.4
.
∎

We conclude this section by using Lemma

5.4
to establish that
the value of a
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma)
function

\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
at a point
p\in{\cal M}

is determined, in a quantified sense, by the set of real numbers

\left\{\sigma(\phi):\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,k}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
where
{\cal T}_{p,k}
is defined in

(
5.19
)
.
The precise result is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5

(Determining Lipschitz Functions at Points)
.

Let
c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and
V
and
W
be real Banach spaces
of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively.
Let
{\cal M}\subset V
be a closed subset, and assume that the
tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible norms
(cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\in V
be a unit
V
-norm basis
of
V
.
For each integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
define
(cf.
(
5.3
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\right\}\subset V^{\otimes n}

(5.57)

and (cf.
(
5.4
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}^{\ord}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\text{ such that }l_{1}\leq\ldots\leq l_{n}\right\}\subset{\cal V}_{n}\subset V^{\otimes n}.

(5.58)

Further, let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{c}\in W
be a unit
W
-norm
basis of
W
, and let

w_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,w_{c}^{\ast}\in W^{\ast}

be the corresponding dual basis of
W^{\ast}
.

Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and let

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
.
For each point
p\in{\cal M}
and each
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}

define
{\cal M}_{p,j}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by

{\cal M}_{p,j}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j=0\\
\left\{\delta_{p,j,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.59)

For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
the linear functional

\delta_{p,0,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,0,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(0)}(p)\right)

(cf.
(
5.15
)
).
For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
and
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
, the linear functional

\delta_{p,j,v,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,j,v,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(j)}(p)[v]\right)

(cf.
(
5.16
)
).
Further, for a given
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
define
{\cal T}_{p,l}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by

{\cal T}_{p,l}:=\bigcup_{j=0}^{l}{\cal M}_{p,j}.

(5.60)

Recall the notation introduced in Remark

2.8
that, for each point

x\in{\cal M}
and each
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we set

\Lambda_{\phi}^{j}(x):=\max_{s\in\{0,\ldots,j\}}\left\{\left|\left|\phi^{(s)}(x)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes s};W)}\right\}.

(5.61)

Then, given
A\geq 0
and
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, the
following implications are valid.
Firstly,

\Lambda_{\phi}^{l}(p)\leq A\implies\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}\right\}\leq A.

(5.62)

Secondly,

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}\right\}\leq A\implies\Lambda_{\phi}^{l}(p)\leq cd^{l}A.

(5.63)

Consequently, in the case that
A=0
, the combination of

(
5.62
)
and
(
5.63
)

yields that

\Lambda_{\phi}^{l}(p)=0\iff\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}\right\}=0.

(5.64)

Therefore the condition that

\phi(p)=\left(\phi^{(0)}(p),\ldots,\phi^{(k)}(p)\right)

vanishes at
p\in{\cal M}
is equivalent to the system of
equations generated by requiring, for every
\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,k}
,
that
\sigma(\phi)=0
; that is, to a system of

\#\left({\cal T}_{p,k}\right)=c\sum_{j=0}^{k}\beta(d,j)

(5.65)

real valued equations.
Here, for integers
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\beta(a,b)
is
defined by (cf.
(
5.1
)
)

\beta(a,b):=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a+b-1\\
b\end{array}\right)=\frac{(a+b-1)!}{(a-1)!b!}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }b=0\\
\frac{1}{b!}\prod_{s=0}^{b-1}(a+s)&\mbox{if }b\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.66)

Proof of Lemma

5.5
.

Let
c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and
V
and
W
be real Banach spaces
of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively.
Let
{\cal M}\subset V
be a closed subset, and assume that the
tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible norms
(cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\in V
be a unit
V
-norm basis
of
V
.
For each integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
define
(cf.
(
5.57
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\right\}\subset V^{\otimes n}

(5.67)

and (cf.
(
5.58
)
)

{\cal V}_{n}^{\ord}:=\left\{v_{l_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l_{n}}:(l_{1},\ldots,l_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{n}\text{ such that }l_{1}\leq\ldots\leq l_{n}\right\}\subset{\cal V}_{n}\subset V^{\otimes n}.

(5.68)

Further, let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{c}\in W
be a unit
W
-norm
basis of
W
, and let

w_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,w_{c}^{\ast}\in W^{\ast}

be the corresponding dual basis of
W^{\ast}
.

Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and let

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
.
For each point
p\in{\cal M}
and each
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}

define
{\cal M}_{p,j}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by
(cf.
(
5.59
)
)

{\cal M}_{p,j}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{\delta_{p,0,s}:s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j=0\\
\left\{\delta_{p,j,v,s}:v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\text{ and }s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}\right\}&\mbox{if }j\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.69)

For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
the linear functional

\delta_{p,0,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,0,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(0)}(p)\right)

(cf.
(
5.15
)
).
For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
and
v\in{\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}
, the linear functional

\delta_{p,j,v,s}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
is
defined, for

F=\left(F^{(0)},\ldots,F^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)
, by

\delta_{p,j,v,s}(F):=w_{s}^{\ast}\left(F^{(j)}(p)[v]\right)

(cf.
(
5.16
)
).
Further, for a given
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
define
{\cal T}_{p,l}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,{\cal M},W)^{\ast}
by
(cf.
(
5.60
)
)

{\cal T}_{p,l}:=\bigcup_{j=0}^{l}{\cal M}_{p,j}.

(5.70)

Recall the notation introduced in Remark

2.8
that, for each point

x\in{\cal M}
and each
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we set
(cf.
(
5.61
)
)

\Lambda_{\phi}^{j}(x):=\max_{s\in\{0,\ldots,j\}}\left\{\left|\left|\phi^{(s)}(x)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes s};W)}\right\}.

(5.71)

We now turn our attention to verifying that both the
implications
(
5.62
)
and

(
5.63
)
are valid.
For this purpose let
A\geq 0
and fix

l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.

First assume that
\Lambda_{\phi}^{l}(p)\leq A
.
A consequence of
(
5.71
)
is
that, for every
j\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
, we have the bound

\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq A
.
In turn, by appealing to Lemma

5.4
,
we deduce, for every
j\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
, that
(cf.
(
5.44
)
)

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}\leq A
.
It now follows from
(
5.70
)
that

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}\right\}\leq A
.
Thus the implication
(
5.62
)
is
valid as claimed.
We could alternatively have established

(
5.62
)
via use of the
inequality
(
5.33
)

established in Lemma
5.3
.

Now assume that

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}\right\}\leq A
.
A consequence of
(
5.70
)
is that,
for every
j\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
, we have

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal M}_{p,j}\right\}\leq A
.
In turn, by appealing to Lemma

5.4
, we deduce, for every

j\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
, that
(cf.
(
5.45
)
)

\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq cd^{j}A
.
It now follows from
(
5.71
)

that
\Lambda_{\phi}^{l}(p)\leq cd^{l}A
.
Thus the implication
(
5.63
)
is
valid as claimed.

The combination of the implication

(
5.62
)
for
A:=0
and the
implication
(
5.63
)
for
A:=0

yield that

\Lambda_{\phi}^{l}(p)=0
if and only if

\max\left\{|\sigma(\phi)|:\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,l}\right\}=0

as claimed in
(
5.64
)
.

To complete the proof, we consider imposing the condition
that
\phi
vanishes at the point
p
.
In particular, this is equivalent to having, for every

j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, that

\left|\left|\phi^{(j)}(p)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}=0
.
Consequently, by appealing to
(
5.64
)
,
this is equivalent to having, for every
\sigma\in{\cal T}_{p,k}
,
that
\sigma(\phi)=0
.
This is a system of
\#\left({\cal T}_{p,k}\right)
real-valued
equations.
The proof will be complete if we can establish that

\#\left({\cal T}_{p,k}\right)=c\sum_{j=0}^{k}\beta(d,j)

as claimed in
(
5.65
)
where,
for integers
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\beta(a,b)
is given by
(cf.
(
5.66
)
)

\beta(a,b):=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a+b-1\\
b\end{array}\right)=\frac{(a+b-1)!}{(a-1)!b!}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }b=0\\
\frac{1}{b!}\prod_{s=0}^{b-1}(a+s)&\mbox{if }b\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(5.72)

We first note via
(
5.70
)
that

\#\left({\cal T}_{p,k}\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\#\left({\cal M}_{p,j}\right)
.
Moreover, for each
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, it follows from

(
5.69
)
that

\#\left({\cal M}_{p,j}\right):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}c&\mbox{if }j=0\\
c\#\left({\cal V}_{j}^{\ord}\right)&\mbox{if }j\geq 1\end{array}\right.\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:beta_ab_def_pf}})}}}{{=}}c\beta(d,j).

(5.73)

Hence
\#\left({\cal T}_{p,k}\right)=c\sum_{j=0}^{k}\beta(d,j)

as claimed in
(
5.65
)
.
This completes the proof of Lemma

5.5
.
∎

6
The HOLGRIM Algorithm

The following

HOLGRIM Extension Step

is used to dynamically grow the collection of
points
P\subset\Sigma
at which we require our
next approximation of
\varphi
to agree with
\varphi
.

HOLGRIM Extension Step

Assume
P^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
. Let
u\in\Span({\cal F})
.
Let
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
and
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}

such that
\#(P^{\prime})+m\leq\Lambda:=\#(\Sigma)
.

\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}:=\bigcup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,q}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}.

(6.1)

First take

\sigma_{1}:=\argmax\left\{\left|\sigma(\varphi-u)\right|:\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right\},

(6.2)

and then take
z_{1}\in\Sigma
to be the point for which

\sigma_{1}\in{\cal T}_{z_{1},q}
.

Inductively for
j=2,3,\ldots,m
take

\sigma_{j}:=\argmax\left\{\left|\sigma(\varphi-u)\right|:\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\setminus\bigcup_{s=1}^{j-1}{\cal T}_{z_{s},q}\right\}

(6.3)

and then take
z_{j}\in\Sigma
to be the point for which

\sigma_{j}\in{\cal T}_{z_{j},q}
.

Once
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\in\Sigma
have been
defined, we extend
P^{\prime}
to

P:=P^{\prime}\cup\left\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\right\}
.

The following
HOLGRIM Recombination Step

details how, for a given subset
P\subset\Sigma

and a given
\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
,
we use recombination to find a function
u\in\Span({\cal F})

satisfying, for every point
z\in P
, that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

HOLGRIM Recombination Step

Assume
P\subset\Sigma
with
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
such
that
m=\#\left(P\right)
.
For integers
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, let
\beta(a,b)
be
as defined in
(
5.1
)
. That is,

\beta(a,b):=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a+b-1\\
b\end{array}\right)=\frac{(a+b-1)!}{(a-1)!b!}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }b=0\\
\frac{1}{b!}\prod_{s=0}^{b-1}(a+s)&\mbox{if }b\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

(6.4)

Let
Q=Q(m,c,d,k)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
be as defined
in
(
5.2
)
. That is,

Q:=1+mc\sum_{r=0}^{k}\beta(d,r).

(6.5)

Let
s\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}

and define
\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{p,q}
.
For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
we
do the following.

(A)

Define
L(k)\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
by

L(k):=\bigcup_{z\in P}{\cal T}_{z,k}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}.

(6.6)

By appealing to Lemma

5.5
we see that,
the cardinality of
L(k)
is
(cf.
(
5.65
)
)

\#\left(L(k)\right)=\sum_{z\in P}\#\left({\cal T}_{z,k}\right)=mc\sum_{j=0}^{k}\beta(d,j)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{lip_k_recomb_eqn_num_recomb_step}})}}}{{=}}Q-1.

(6.7)

(B)

Let
L_{j}(k)\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}

be the subset resulting
from applying a random permutation to the ordering
of the linear functionals in
L(k)
.

(C)

Apply recombination via Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
, with
the Banach space
X
, the integers
{\cal N},m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
the subset
L\subset X^{\ast}
, the finite subset

{\cal F}\subset X
,
and the target
\varphi\in\Span({\cal F})\subset X
in that result
as
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
,
{\cal N},Q-1\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

L_{j}(k)
, and

\varphi\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
here
respectively, to find
u_{j}\in\Span({\cal F})
satisfying

\max\left\{\left|\sigma(\varphi-u_{j})\right|:\sigma\in L_{j}(k)\right\}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}.

(6.8)

Since
L_{j}(k)
is a re-ordering of
L(k)
,
the combination of
(
6.6
)

and Lemma
5.5

for the subset
{\cal M}
there as
P
here
(specifically implication
(
5.63
)
)
yields, for each point
z\in P
, that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u_{j}}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

(D)

Compute

E[u_{j}]:=\max\left\{\left|\sigma(\varphi-u_{j})\right|:\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right\}.

(6.9)

After obtaining the functions
u_{1},\ldots,u_{s}\in\Span({\cal F})
we define
u\in\Span({\cal F})
by

u:=\argmin\left\{E[u_{j}]:j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}\right\}.

(6.10)

Then
u\in\Span({\cal F})
is returned as our approximation
of
\varphi
that satisfies, for every
z\in P
, that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

Theoretically, there is no problem choosing
\varepsilon_{0}:=0

in the
HOLGRIM Recombination Step
. The use of
recombination in Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
does theoretically
find an approximation

u
of
\varphi
such that, for every
\sigma\in L_{j}(k)
,
we have
\sigma(\varphi-u)=0
.
Hence, via Lemma
5.5

(cf.
(
5.64
)
for
l
there are

k
here),
we may theoretically conclude that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)=0

for all points
z\in P
for the given subset

P\subset\Sigma
.
However, implementations of recombination inevitably result
in numerical errors.
That is, the returned coefficients will only solve the
equations modulo some (ideally) small error term.
Similarly to
[
LM22-I
]
, we account for this in our
analysis by only assuming in

(
6.8
)

that the resulting
approximation
u_{j}\in\Span({\cal F})
only satisfies,
for every
\sigma\in L_{j}(k)
, that

|\sigma(\varphi-u_{j})|\leq\varepsilon_{0}/cd^{k}

for some (small) constant
\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
.
The inclusion of the
cd^{k}
factor on the denominator is
to ensure that, via an application of implication

(
5.63
)
in Lemma

5.5
for the subset

{\cal M}
there as
P
here,
for every point
z\in P
we have that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

We exploit the potential advantage offered by shuffling
the order of the inputs for recombination that is utilised
in
[
LM22-I
]
.
That is, if recombination is applied to
a linear system of equations corresponding to a matrix
A
,
then a Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) of the matrix
A

is used to find a basis of the kernel
\ker(A)

(see Section 3 in
[
LM22-I
]
, for example).
Consequently, re-ordering the rows of the matrix
(i.e. changing the order in which the equations are
considered) can potentially result in a different
basis for
\ker(A)
being selected.
Hence shuffling the order of the equations can affect the
approximation returned by recombination via Lemma 3.1 in

[
LM22-I
]
.
As done in
[
LM22-I
]
, we exploit this by optimising
the approximation returned by recombination over a
chosen number of shuffles of the
equations forming the linear system.

We now detail our proposed
HOLGRIM
algorithm to find an
approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})
of
\varphi\in\Span({\cal F})

that is close to
\varphi
throughout
\Sigma
in the sense
that, for a given
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we have

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon
for every point

z\in\Sigma
.

HOLGRIM

(A)

Fix
\varepsilon>0
as the
target accuracy
threshold
,
\varepsilon_{0}\in[0,\varepsilon)

as the
acceptable recombination error
,

M\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
as the

maximum number of steps
, and

q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
as the
order level
.
Choose integers
s_{1},\ldots,s_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}

as the
shuffle numbers
, and integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
with

\kappa:=k_{1}+\ldots+k_{M}\leq\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}.

(6.11)

Choose
scaling factors

A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for every

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, we have

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.

(B)

For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, if

a_{i}<0
then replace
a_{i}
and
f_{i}
by

-a_{i}
and
-f_{i}
respectively.
This ensures that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}>0

whilst leaving the expansion

\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
unaltered.
Additionally, for each

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
replace
f_{i}
by

h_{i}:=f_{i}/A_{i}

so that
h_{i}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
with

||h_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq 1
,
and that

\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}h_{i}

where

\alpha_{i}:=a_{i}A_{i}
.

(C)

Apply the
HOLGRIM Extension Step
,
with
P^{\prime}:=\varnothing
,

u\equiv 0
and
m:=k_{1}
, to obtain a
subset
\Sigma_{1}=\left\{z_{1,1},\ldots,z_{1,k_{1}}\right\}\subset\Sigma
.
Apply the
HOLGRIM Recombination Step
,
with
P:=\Sigma_{1}
and
s:=s_{1}
, to find a
function
u_{1}\in\Span({\cal F})
satisfying, for
every point
z\in\Sigma_{1}
, that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u_{1}}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

If
M=1
then the algorithm terminates here and
returns
u_{1}
as the final approximation of
\varphi
.

(D)

If
M\geq 2
then we proceed inductively for

t\geq 2
as follows.
If
|\sigma(\varphi-u_{t-1})|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}

for every linear functional
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}

then we stop and return
u_{t-1}
as the final
approximation of
\varphi
.
Otherwise, we apply the

HOLGRIM Extension Step
with

P^{\prime}:=\Sigma_{t-1}
,

u:=u_{t-1}
and
m:=k_{t}
, to obtain a
subset
\Sigma_{t}=\Sigma_{t-1}\cup\left\{z_{t,1},\ldots,z_{t,k_{t}}\right\}\subset\Sigma
.
Apply the
HOLGRIM Recombination Step
,
with
P:=\Sigma_{t}
and
s:=s_{t}
, to find a
function
u_{t}\in\Span({\cal F})
satisfying, for
every point
z\in\Sigma_{t}
, that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u_{t}}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

The algorithm ends either by returning
u_{t-1}

for
t\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
for which the stopping
criterion was triggered as the final approximation of

\varphi
, or by returning
u_{M}
as the final
approximation of
\varphi
if the stopping criterion is
never triggered.

We claim that if
M\geq 2
and the

HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates
before

the final
M^{\text{th}}
step is completed, then the
resulting approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})
of
\varphi

satisfies, for every
z\in\Sigma
, that

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon
.
To see this, let
m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

and suppose that the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
terminates during the
m^{\text{th}}
step,
i.e. that the
m^{\text{th}}
step is not completed.
Then
u_{m-1}\in\Span({\cal F})
is the approximation of

\varphi
returned by the
HOLGRIM
algorithm.

It follows from Step
(D)
in the

HOLGRIM
algorithm that this requires that

|\sigma(\varphi-u_{m-1})|\leq\varepsilon/cd^{q}
for
every
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}
.
Recall that
\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,q}
.
Thus, for any
z\in\Sigma
, we must have
that
|\sigma(\varphi-u_{m-1})|\leq\varepsilon/cd^{q}
for
every
\sigma\in{\cal T}_{z,q}
.
Consequently, by appealing to Lemma

5.5

(specifically to the implication

(
5.63
)
),
we conclude that
\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{m-1}}(z)\leq\varepsilon
.
Since
z\in\Sigma
was arbitrary we have established
that
\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{m-1}}(z)\right\}\leq\varepsilon

as claimed.

7
Complexity Cost

In this section we establish an estimate for the complexity cost
of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm detailed in Section

6
.
We first record the complexity cost of the

HOLGRIM Extension Step
.

Lemma 7.1

(
HOLGRIM Extension Step
Complexity Cost)
.

Let
{\cal N},\Lambda,m,t,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
V
and
W
be real Banach spaces of dimensions
d
and

c
respectively.
Assume the tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible
norms (cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
\Sigma\subset V
be a finite subset with cardinality

\Lambda
.
Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and let
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Define
\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,q}
.
Let

{\cal F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and define

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
.
Assume that the subset

\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
,
the subset

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
have already been computed.
Then for any subset
P^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
with

\#\left(P^{\prime}\right)+m\leq\Lambda
and any

u\in\Span({\cal F})
with
\#{\rm support}(u)=t
,
the complexity cost of applying the

HOLGRIM Extension Step
, with the
P^{\prime}
,
u
, and
m

there as the
P^{\prime}
,
u
, and
m
here respectively, is

{\cal O}\left(c(m+t)\Lambda D(d,q)\right)
.

Proof of Lemma

7.1
.

Let
{\cal N},\Lambda,m,t,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
V
and
W
be real Banach spaces of dimensions
d
and

c
respectively.
Assume the tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible
norms (cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
\Sigma\subset V
be a finite subset with cardinality

\Lambda
.
Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and let
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Define
\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,q}
.
Let

{\cal F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and define

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
.
Assume that the subset

\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
,
the subset

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
have already been computed.
Suppose that
P^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
with

\#\left(P^{\prime}\right)+m\leq\Lambda
and

u\in\Span({\cal F})
with
\#{\rm support}(u)=t
.
Recall our convention that
{\rm support}(u)
is the set of the
functions
f_{i}
that correspond to a non-zero coefficient in
the expansion of
u
in terms of the
f_{i}
. That is,

u=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}u_{i}f_{i}
for real numbers

u_{1},\ldots,u_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and

{\rm support}(u):=\left\{f_{j}:j\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}\text{ and }u_{j}\neq 0\right\}.

(7.1)

Consider carrying out the
HOLGRIM Extension Step

with the
P^{\prime}
,
u
, and
m
there are
P^{\prime}
,
u
, and
m

here respectively.
Since we have access to
\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right\}
and

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right\}

for every
i\in\{1,\dots,{\cal N}\}
without additional
computation, and since
\#{\rm support}(u)=t
, the complexity
cost of computing the set

\left\{\left|\sigma(\varphi-u)\right|:\sigma\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right\}

is no worse than

{\cal O}\left(t\#\left(\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\right)\right)={\cal O}\left(ct\Lambda D(d,q)\right)
.
Each of the
m
argmax values are found from a list of no
greater than
\#\left(\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}\right)=c\Lambda D(d,q)

real numbers, and so the complexity cost of extracting these

m
argmax values is no worse than

{\cal O}\left(cm\Lambda D(d,q)\right)
.
Moreover, for
j\in\{2,\ldots,m\}
, the list from which we
extract the next the
j^{\text{th}}
argmax value
is obtained by removing
cD(d,q)
values from the previous
list, i.e. for every linear functional
\sigma\in{\cal T}_{z_{j-1},q}

we remove the value
\sigma(\varphi-u)
,
which results in removing
cD(d,q)
values in total
since this is the cardinality of
{\cal T}_{z_{j-1},q}

(cf.
(
5.20
)
).
Thus the complexity cost of the discarding is, in total, no
worse than
{\cal O}\left(cmD(d,q)\right)

The complexity cost of appending the resulting
m
points

z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\in\Sigma
to the collection
P^{\prime}
is

{\cal O}(m)
.
Therefore the entire
HOLGRIM Extension Step
has a
complexity cost no worse than

{\cal O}\left(c(m+t)\Lambda D(d,q)\right)

as claimed.
This completes the proof of Lemma
7.1
.
∎

We next record the complexity cost of the

HOLGRIM Recombination Step
.

Lemma 7.2

(
HOLGRIM Recombination Step

Complexity Cost)
.

Let
{\cal N},\Lambda,m,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
V
and
W
be real Banach spaces of dimensions
d
and

c
respectively.
Assume the tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible
norms (cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
\Sigma\subset V
be a finite subset with cardinality

\Lambda
.
Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and let
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Define
\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,q}

and
\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,k}
.
Let

{\cal F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and define

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
.
Assume that the subset

\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}^{\ast}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
,
the subset

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
have already been computed.
Then for any
P\subset\Sigma
with cardinality
\#(P)=m

the complexity cost of applying the

HOLGRIM Recombination Step
, with the subset
P
, the
integer
s
, and the order level
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
there
as
P
,
s
, and
q
here respectively, is

{\cal O}\left(smcD(d,k)({\cal N}+cD(d,q)\Lambda)+sm^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{mcD(d,k)}\right)\right).

(7.2)

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

Let
{\cal N},\Lambda,m,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
V
and
W
be real Banach spaces of dimensions
d
and

c
respectively.
Assume the tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with admissible
norms (cf. Definition
2.1
).
Let
\Sigma\subset V
be a finite subset with cardinality

\Lambda
.
Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and let
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Define
\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,q}

and
\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,k}
.
Let

{\cal F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and define

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
.
Assume that the subset

\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}^{\ast}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
,
the subset

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
have already been computed.
Let
P\subset\Sigma
have cardinality
\#(P)=m
.

Consider applying the

HOLGRIM Recombination Step
with the subset
P
the
integer
s
, and the order level
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
there
as
P
,
s
, and
q
here respectively.
Define (cf.
(
6.6
)
)

L(k):=\cup_{z\in P}{\cal T}_{z,k}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
.
Since
\#(P)=m
, it follows that (cf.

(
6.7
)

the cardinality of
L(k)
is

\#\left(L(k)\right)=mcD(d,k)
.

Consider a fixed
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
.
The complexity cost of shuffling of the elements in

L(k)
to obtain
L_{j}(k)
in

HOLGRIM Recombination Step

(B)
is

{\cal O}(mcD(d,k))
.

In
HOLGRIM Recombination Step

(C)
, recombination is applied via
Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
.
In particular, Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
is applied with
the Banach space
X
, the integers
{\cal N},m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
the subset
L\subset X^{\ast}
, the finite subset

{\cal F}\subset X
,
and the target
\varphi\in\Span({\cal F})\subset X
in that result
as
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
,
{\cal N},mcD(d,k)+1\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

L_{j}(k)
, and

\varphi\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
here
respectively, to find
u_{j}\in\Span({\cal F})
satisfying

\max\left\{\left|\sigma(\varphi-u_{j})\right|:\sigma\in L_{j}(k)\right\}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}.

(7.3)

It is established in Lemma 3.1
[
LM22-I
]
that the
complexity cost of this application is

{\cal O}\left({\cal N}mcD(d,k)+m^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{mcD(d,k)}\right)\right).

(7.4)

Further, since
\#\left(L_{j}(k)\right)=mcD(d,k)
,
it follows from Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
that the cardinality
of
{\rm support}(u_{j})
satisfies

\#{\rm support}(u_{j})\leq mcD(d,k)+1
.
Thus, since we already have access to

\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}^{\ast}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
,
the subset

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
without additional computation and

\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\subset\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}
,
the complexity cost of computing

E[u_{j}]:=\max\left\{|\sigma(\varphi-u_{j})|:\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\right\}

is

{\cal O}\left(mcD(d,k)\#\left(\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\right)\right)={\cal O}\left(mc^{2}\Lambda D(d,k)D(d,q)\right).

(7.5)

Therefore, the combination of

(
7.4
)

and

(
7.5
)

yields that the complexity cost of carrying out

HOLGRIM Recombination Step

(A)
,

(B)
,
(C)
, and

(D)
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}

is

{\cal O}\left(smc^{2}\Lambda D(d,k)D(d,q)+s{\cal N}mcD(d,k)+sm^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{mcD(d,k)}\right)\right)=\\
{\cal O}\left(smcD(d,k)\left({\cal N}+c\Lambda D(d,q)\right)+sm^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{mcD(d,k)}\right)\right)

(7.6)

The complexity cost of the final selection of

u:=\argmin\left\{E[w]:w\in\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{s}\}\right\}

is
{\cal O}(s)
.
Combined with
(
7.6
)
, this yields
that the complexity cost of the entire

HOLGRIM Recombination Step
is

{\cal O}\left(smcD(d,k)\left({\cal N}+c\Lambda D(d,q)\right)+sm^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{mcD(d,k)}\right)\right).

(7.7)

as claimed in
(
7.2
)
.
This completes the proof of Lemma

7.2
.
∎

We now establish an upper bound for the complexity cost of
the
HOLGRIM
algorithm via repeated use of
Lemmas
7.1
and

7.2
.
This is the content of the following result.

Lemma 7.3

(
HOLGRIM Complexity Cost
)
.

Let
{\cal N},M,\Lambda,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and

\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
.
Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
.
Let
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Take
s_{1},\ldots,s_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
with

\kappa:=k_{1}+\ldots+k_{M}\leq\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}.

(7.8)

For
j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
let
\tau_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}k_{i}
.
Let
V
and
W
be real finite-dimensional Banach spaces
of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively.
Assume that the tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with
admissible norms (cf. Definition
2.1
),
and let
\Sigma\subset V
be a finite subset of cardinality

\Lambda
. Let

{\cal F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\setminus\{0\}
.
Fix a choice of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for
every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, we have

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and define

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
.
Then the complexity cost of applying the
HOLGRIM

algorithm to approximate
\varphi
, with
\varepsilon
as the target
accuracy,
\varepsilon_{0}
as the acceptable recombination error,

M
as the maximum number of steps,
q
as the order level,

s_{1},\ldots,s_{M}
as the shuffle numbers, the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
chosen in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and the real numbers

A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}
as the
scaling factors
chosen
in
HOLGRIM

(A)
, is

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda+\sum_{j=1}^{M}cs_{j}\tau_{j}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+cD(d,q)\Lambda\right)+s_{j}\tau_{j}^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{c\tau_{j}D(d,k)}\right)\right).

(7.9)

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

Let
{\cal N},M,\Lambda,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and

\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
.
Let
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
.
Let
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Take
s_{1},\ldots,s_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
with

\kappa:=k_{1}+\ldots+k_{M}\leq\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}.

(7.10)

For
j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
let
\tau_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}k_{i}
.
Let
V
and
W
be real finite-dimensional Banach spaces
of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively.
Assume that the tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with
admissible norms (cf. Definition
2.1
),
and let
\Sigma\subset V
be a finite subset of cardinality

\Lambda
. Let

{\cal F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\setminus\{0\}
.
Fix a choice of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for
every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, we have

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.
Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and define

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
.

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM

algorithm to approximate
\varphi
with
\varepsilon
as the target
accuracy,
\varepsilon_{0}
as the acceptable recombination error,

M
as the maximum number of steps,
q
as the order level,

s_{1},\ldots,s_{M}
as the shuffle numbers, the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
chosen in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and the real numbers

A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}
as the
scaling factors
chosen
in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.

Since the cardinality of
{\cal F}
is
{\cal N}
, the complexity cost of
the rescaling and sign alterations in Step
(B)

of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm is
{\cal O}({\cal N})
.
The complexity cost of computing the sets

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\right\}

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
is

{\cal O}\left(c{\cal N}\Lambda D(d,k)\right)
.
Subsequently, having access to the sets

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\right\}

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
means that
the complexity cost of computing the set

\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\right\}
is

{\cal O}(c{\cal N}\Lambda D(d,k))
.
Consequently, the total complexity cost of performing these
computations is
{\cal O}(c{\cal N}\Lambda D(d,k))
.

We appeal to Lemma
7.1

to conclude that the complexity cost of performing the

HOLGRIM Extension Step
as in Step
(C)

of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm (i.e. with

P^{\prime}:=\emptyset
,
u:=0
, and
m:=k_{1}
) is

{\cal O}\left(ck_{1}\Lambda D(d,k)\right)
.
An application of Lemma
7.2
,
yields that the complexity cost of the use of
the
HOLGRIM Recombination Step
in Step

(C)

of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
(i.e. with the subset
P:=\Sigma_{1}
and the shuffle number

s:=s_{1}
) is

{\cal O}\left(cs_{1}k_{1}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+c\Lambda D(d,q)\right)+s_{1}c^{3}k_{1}^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left({\cal N}/ck_{1}D(d,k)\right)\right)
.
Hence the complexity cost of Step
(C)

of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm is

{\cal O}\left(cs_{1}k_{1}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+c\Lambda D(d,q)\right)+s_{1}c^{3}k_{1}^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{ck_{1}D(d,k)}\right)\right)
.

In the case that
M=1
we can already conclude that the
complexity cost of performing the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
is

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda+cs_{1}k_{1}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+c\Lambda D(d,q)\right)+s_{1}c^{3}k_{1}^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{ck_{1}D(d,k)}\right)\right)

(7.11)

as claimed in
(
7.9
)
.
Now suppose that
M\geq 2
. We assume that all
M
steps
of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm are completed without
early termination since this is the case that will maximise
the complexity cost.
Under this assumption, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}

let
u_{j}\in\Span({\cal F})
denote the approximation of
\varphi

returned after step
j
of the

HOLGRIM
algorithm is completed.
Recall that
\tau_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}k_{i}
.
The approximation
u_{j}
is
obtained by applying recombination via Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]

to find an approximation
that is within
\varepsilon_{0}
of
\varphi
on a subset of

c\tau_{j}D(d,k)
linear functionals from
\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}

(cf. Step
(D)
of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm).
Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
therefore ensures that

\#{\rm support}(u_{j})\leq 1+c\tau_{j}D(d,k)
.
In order to establish an upper bound for the maximal complexity
cost, we assume that we are in the most costly situation
in which
\#{\rm support}(u_{j})=1+c\tau_{j}D(d,k)
.

Let
t\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
and consider
Step
(D)
of the
HOLGRIM

algorithm for the
s
there as
t
here.
Since
\#{\rm support}(u_{t-1})=1+c\tau_{t-1}D(d,k)

and we have already computed the set

\left\{\sigma(\varphi):\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}^{\ast}\right\}

and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, the set

\left\{\sigma(f_{i}):\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\right\}
,
the complexity cost of computing the set

\left\{\left|\sigma(\varphi-u_{t-1})\right|:\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\right\}
for the purpose of
checking the termination criterion is

{\cal O}\left((1+c\tau_{t-1}D(d,k))\#\left(\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}\right)\right)={\cal O}\left(c^{2}\tau_{t-1}D(d,q)D(d,k)\Lambda\right)
.
Since
\#{\rm support}(u_{t-1})=1+c\tau_{t-1}D(d,k)
,
Lemma
7.1
tells us that
the complexity cost of performing the

HOLGRIM Extension Step
as in Step
(D)

of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm (i.e. with

P^{\prime}:=\Sigma_{t-1}
,
u:=u_{t-1}
,
and
m:=k_{t}
) is

{\cal O}\left(c\left(k_{t}+c\tau_{t}D(d,k)\right)\Lambda D(d,q)\right)={\cal O}\left(c^{2}\tau_{t}D(d,k)D(d,q)\Lambda\right)
.
Lemma
7.2
yield that
the complexity cost of the use of the

HOLGRIM Recombination Step
in Step
(D)

of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
(i.e. with
P:=\Sigma_{t}
and
s:=s_{t}
) is

{\cal O}\left(c\tau_{t}s_{t}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+c\Lambda D(d,q)\right)+s_{t}c^{3}\tau_{t}^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{c\tau_{t}D(d,k)}\right)\right)
.
Hence the entirety of Step
(D)

(for
t
here playing the role of
s
there)
has a complexity cost of

{\cal O}\left(cs_{t}\tau_{t}D(d,k){\cal N}+c^{2}s_{t}\tau_{t}D(d,q)D(d,k)\Lambda+s_{t}\tau_{t}^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{c\tau_{t}D(d,k)}\right)\right).

(7.12)

Summing together the complexity costs arising in

(
7.12
)
for each

t\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
yields that the complexity cost
of carrying out step
(D)
of the

HOLGRIM
algorithm for every
t\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

is

{\cal O}\left(\sum_{j=2}^{M}cs_{j}\tau_{j}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+cD(d,q)\Lambda\right)+s_{j}\tau_{j}^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{c\tau_{j}D(d,k)}\right)\right).

(7.13)

Having previously established the complexity cost of
carrying out Steps

(A)
,
(B)
, and
(C)

of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm in

(
7.11
)
,
the combination of
(
7.11
)

and
(
7.13
)
yields that
the complexity cost
of performing the entire
HOLGRIM
algorithm is
(recalling that
\tau_{1}:=k_{1}
)

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda+\sum_{j=1}^{M}cs_{j}\tau_{j}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+cD(d,q)\Lambda\right)+s_{j}\tau_{j}^{3}c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{c\tau_{j}D(d,k)}\right)\right)

(7.14)

as claimed in
(
7.9
)
.
This completes the proof of Lemma

7.3
.
∎

We end this section by explicitly recording the complexity
cost upper bound arising in Lemma

7.3
for some particular
parameter choices.
In both examples we assume that

1+cD(d,k)\Lambda<{\cal N}
.

First consider the choices that
M:=1
,
k_{1}:=\Lambda
and

s_{1}:=1
.
This corresponds to making a single application of recombination
to find an approximation
u
of
\varphi
that is within
\varepsilon_{0}

of
\varphi
throughout
\Sigma
in the pointwise sense that,
for every
z\in\Sigma
, we have

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.
Lemma
7.3
yields that the
complexity cost of doing this is

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda+c^{2}D(d,q)D(d,k)\Lambda^{2}+c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\Lambda^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{cD(d,k)\Lambda}\right)\right)
.

Secondly consider the choices that

M\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
, and arbitrary fixed

s_{1},\ldots,s_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
P\subset\Sigma
denote the collection of points that is
inductively grown in the
HOLGRIM
algorithm.
These choices correspond to adding a single new points from

\Sigma
to
P
at each step of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm.
For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
we have

\tau_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}k_{i}=j
.
Lemma
7.3
yields that the
complexity cost of doing this is

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda+\sum_{j=1}^{M}cs_{j}jD(d,k)\left({\cal N}+cD(d,q)\Lambda\right)+s_{j}c^{3}j^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{cjD(d,k)}\right)\right)
.
If we further restrict to only allowing a single application of
recombination at each step (i.e. impose that

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1
) then the complexity cost is

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda+cM^{2}D(d,k)\left({\cal N}+cD(d,q)\Lambda\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{M}c^{3}j^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{cjD(d,k)}\right)\right)
.

In particular, if we take
M:=\Lambda
(which corresponds
to allowing for the possibility that the subset
P
eventually
becomes the entire set
\Sigma
) then the complexity cost is

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda^{2}+c^{2}D(d,q)D(d,k)\Lambda^{3}+\sum_{j=1}^{\Lambda}c^{3}j^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{cjD(d,k)}\right)\right).

(7.15)

If the integer
{\cal N}
is large enough that

e^{1/3}cD(d,k)\Lambda<{\cal N}

then for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,\Lambda\}
we have
the estimate

c^{3}j^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\log({\cal N}/cjD(d,k))\leq c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\Lambda^{3}\log({\cal N}/cD(d,k)\Lambda)
.
Thus, under these assumptions, the complexity cost
in
(
7.15
)
is no
worse than

{\cal O}\left(cD(d,k){\cal N}\Lambda^{2}+c^{2}D(d,q)D(d,k)\Lambda^{3}+c^{3}D(d,k)^{3}\Lambda^{4}\log\left(\frac{{\cal N}}{cD(d,k)\Lambda}\right)\right).

(7.16)

8
Theoretical Guarantees Inherited from GRIM

In this section we discuss the convergence guarantees that
can be established for the
HOLGRIM
algorithm by
directly appealing to the convergence theory for the

Banach GRIM
algorithm developed in Section 6
of
[
LM22-I
]
.
We discuss the limitations of this approach and
motivate the distinct approach using the

Lipschitz Sandwich Theorems
established in
[
LM24
]

to obtain convergence results for the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
that is adopted in Section
10
.

Let
c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
V
be a
d
-dimensional real Banach
space, and
W
be a
c
-dimensional real Banach space.
Suppose that the tensor powers of
V
are all equipped with
admissible norms (cf. Definition
2.1
)
and that
\Sigma\subset V
is a finite subset of cardinality

\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
{\cal N}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and
\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}

such that
\gamma\in(k,k+1]
.
Let
D=D(d,k)
be the constant defined in

(
5.2
)
; that is

D(d,k):=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\beta(d,l)
where
\beta(d,0):=1
and,
for each

l\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
if
k\geq 1
,

\beta(d,l):=\frac{(d+l-1)\dots d}{l!}

(cf.
(
5.1
)
).

Suppose, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, that

f_{i}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
is not identically zero
and define a finite subset

{\cal F}:=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
.
For non-zero coefficients

a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}

consider
\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
.
For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
fix a choice of

A_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.
Finally, let
\varepsilon>0
and
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
and fix a choice of
\varepsilon_{0}\in[0,\varepsilon/cd^{q})
.

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
approximate
\varphi
throughout
\Sigma
with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}

in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,
the target accuracy
in
HOLGRIM

(A)
as
\varepsilon
,
the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
as
\varepsilon_{0}
,
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
as
q
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1
as the shuffle numbers in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}
in
HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
as the scaling factors in

HOLGRIM

(A)
.

Following
HOLGRIM

(B)
, for each

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
replace
a_{i}
and
f_{i}
by

-a_{i}
and
-f_{i}
if the original
a_{i}
satisfies

a_{i}<0
.
Subsequently, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
,
define
h_{i}:=f_{i}/A_{i}
so that

h_{i}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
with

||h_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq 1
.
If, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, we now define

\alpha_{i}:=a_{i}A_{i}>0
, then

\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}h_{i}
.

We briefly outline how one could directly
apply the convergence results for the
Banach GRIM

algorithm established in Section 6 of
[
LM22-I
]
.
For this purpose momentarily assume, for some integer

n\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
, that

L=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\}\subset\Sigma
is the collection
of points in
\Sigma
that have been selected during the

HOLGRIM
algorithm.
Let
u_{n}\in\Span({\cal F})
denote the approximation found by
recombination, via the
HOLGRIM Recombination Step
,
at step
n
.
Then we have that for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}

and for every linear functional

\sigma\in{\cal T}_{z_{j},k}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}

that
|\sigma(\varphi-u_{n})|\leq\varepsilon_{0}/cd^{k}
.
Moreover, since the cardinality of

T_{n}:=\cup_{j=1}^{n}{\cal T}_{z_{j},k}
is

ncD(d,k)
(cf.
(
5.20
)
),
it follows from Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
that, for

Q_{n}:=1+ncD(d,k)
, there are
coefficients
b_{1},\ldots,b_{Q_{n}}\geq 0
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}

for which
u_{n}=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{n}}b_{s}h_{e(s)}
, and
such that
\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{n}}b_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}=C
.
Consequently
||u_{n}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq C
.

Thus we have all the hypotheses required to apply
Lemma 6.4 in
[
LM22-I
]
for the choices of

L
,
C
,
\theta
, and
\theta_{0}
there as

T_{n}:=\cup_{j=1}^{n}{\cal T}_{z_{j},k}
,
C
,
\varepsilon/cd^{q}
, and

\varepsilon_{0}/cd^{k}
here respectively.
By doing so, we conclude that whenever

\sigma\in\Reach_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{n}-1}\right)}}\left(T_{n},2C,\varepsilon/cd^{q},\varepsilon_{0}/cd^{k}\right)

we have
|\sigma(\varphi-u_{n})|\leq\varepsilon/cd^{q}
.
Here we use the notation from Subsection 6.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
that

\Reach_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{n}-1}\right)}}\left(T_{n},2C,\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}\right):=\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bigcup_{0<r<\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{0}}d^{k-q}}\ovSpan_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{n}-1}\right)}}\left(T_{n},r\right)_{\frac{1}{2C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}r\right)}&\mbox{if }\varepsilon_{0}\neq 0\\
\Span\left(T_{n}\right)_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2Ccd^{q}}}&\mbox{if }\varepsilon_{0}=0\end{array}\right.

(8.1)

with

\Span_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{n}-1}\right)}}(T_{n},r):=\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in T_{n}}a(\sigma)\sigma~:~\forall\sigma\in T_{n}\text{ we have }a(\sigma)\in{\mathbb{R}}\text{ and }\sum_{\sigma\in T_{n}}|a(\sigma)|\leq r\right\}.

(8.2)

Finally, the subscripts in
(
8.1
)
denote
the fattening of the subset in
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
.
To elaborate, this means that

\ovSpan_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{n}-1}\right)}}(T_{n},r)_{\frac{1}{2C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}r\right)}

denotes the collection of linear functionals

\sigma\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
for which there
exists a linear functional

\rho\in\ovSpan_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{n}-1}\right)}}(T_{n},r)

whose distance to
\sigma
in
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
is no
greater than
\frac{1}{2C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}r\right)
.
Similarly,

\Span(T_{n})_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2Ccd^{q}}}

denotes the collection of linear functionals

\sigma\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
for which there
exists
\rho\in\Span(T_{n})

whose distance to
\sigma
in
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
is no
greater than
\frac{\varepsilon}{2Ccd^{q}}
.

Consider the choice of the next point
z_{n+1}\in\Sigma

made by the
HOLGRIM
algorithm.
An examination of Step
(D)
of the
HOLGRIM

algorithm reveals that if the algorithm does not terminate
before the next point is selected, then there is a linear
functional
\sigma_{n+1}\in\Sigma_{q}^{\ast}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,q}
for which

|\sigma_{n+1}(\varphi-u_{n})|>\varepsilon/cd^{q}
, and the point

z_{n+1}\in\Sigma
is chosen to be such that

\sigma_{n+1}\in{\cal T}_{z_{n+1},q}
.
As a consequence, we have that

\sigma_{n+1}\notin\Reach_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{n}-1}\right)}}\left(T_{n},2C,\varepsilon/cd^{q},\varepsilon_{0}/cd^{k}\right)

Therefore, by following the strategy of Section 6 in
[
LM22-I
]

verbatim, we could establish that the integer

N:=\max\left\{d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}:\begin{array}[]{c}\text{There exists }z_{1},\ldots,z_{d}\in\Sigma\text{ such that for every }j\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}\\
\text{there exists }\sigma\in{\cal T}_{z_{j+1},q}\text{ with }\sigma\notin\Reach_{||\cdot||_{l^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{Q_{j}-1}\right)}}\left(T_{j},2C,\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}\right)\end{array}\right\}

(8.3)

is an upper bound for the maximum number of steps that the

HOLGRIM
algorithm can complete before terminating.
The upper bound given in

(
8.3
)
suffers the
following issues.

Firstly, the integer
N
defined in

(
8.3
)
is determined
by geometrical properties of the subset

\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}:=\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\cal T}_{z,k}

of the dual space
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
.
Computing
N
, or computing any of the geometric
quantities that are established to provide upper bounds for

N
in Subsection 6.2 of
[
LM22-I
]
, requires computing
the distance between the linear functionals in
\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}

in the dual space
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
.
An immediate issue is how to compute such distances. The
distance in the dual space
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
between

\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
is given by

\sup\left\{|\sigma_{1}(\phi)-\sigma_{2}(\phi)|:\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\text{ with }||\phi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq 1\right\}.

(8.4)

It is not immediately clear how to compute the supremum
in
(
8.4
)
in a cost-effective way.

Secondly, it is not clear how the distance in

(
8.4
)
for
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}
relates to the distance between the points

z_{1},z_{2}\in\Sigma
for which
\sigma_{1}\in{\cal T}_{z_{1},k}

and
\sigma_{2}\in{\cal T}_{z_{2},k}
.
The data of interest in our problem is the points in
\Sigma
;
the linear functionals in
\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}
are introduced to
enable our use of recombination
(cf.
HOLGRIM Recombination Step
).
Consequently we would prefer upper bounds for the number of
steps that the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can complete before
terminating to depend on the geometry of the points

\Sigma\subset V
rather than the geometry of the linear
functionals
\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}
.
But it is not clear how the
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)^{\ast}

distances involved in the definition of
N
in

(
8.3
)
can be converted
to distances between the points in
\Sigma
themselves.

In Section
10
,
we circumvent both these issues by utilising the

Lipschitz Sandwich Theorems
established in
[
LM24
]

to obtain an upper bound on the maximum number of steps that
the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can complete without terminating
that both depends on the geometry of the points
\Sigma
rather
than the linear functionals
\Sigma^{\ast}_{k}
, and can be
estimated knowing only the distances between the points in

\Sigma
.

9
Point Separation Lemma

In this section we establish that the points selected
during the
HOLGRIM
algorithm, under the choices that

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}

and, for every integer
t\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, that

s_{t}:=1
and
k_{t}:=1
, have to be
a definite
V
-distance apart.
This is the content of the following result.

Lemma 9.1

(
HOLGRIM Point Separation
)
.

Let
{\cal N},\Lambda,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and fix a choice of
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Let
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
be real numbers such that

0\leq\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}
.
Assume
V
and
W
are finite dimensional real Banach
spaces, of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively,
with
\Sigma\subset V
a finite subset of cardinality

\Lambda
.
Assume that the tensor products of
V
are all
equipped with admissible norms (cf. Definition

2.1
).
Assume that for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}

the element
f_{i}=\left(f_{i}^{(0)},\ldots,f_{i}^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
is non-zero
and define
{\cal F}:=\left\{f_{i}~:~i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
.
Choose scalars
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such
that, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, we have

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.
Given
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
,
define
\varphi=\left(\varphi^{(0)},\ldots,\varphi^{(k)}\right)\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

and
C>0
by

({\bf I})\quad\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi^{(l)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f^{(l)}_{i}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad({\bf II})\quad C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\left|a_{i}\right|A_{i}>0.

(9.1)

Then there exists a positive constant

r=r(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)>0
, given by

r:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0~:~2C\lambda^{\gamma-q}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\lambda}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}\right\},

(9.2)

for which the following is true.

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
approximate
\varphi
throughout
\Sigma
with the choices
of
M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}
,
the target accuracy threshold in
HOLGRIM

(A)

as
\theta
, the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
as
\theta_{0}
, the order level in

HOLGRIM

(A)
as
b
,
the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
as
s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1
, the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)

as
k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
, and
the scaling factors in
HOLGRIM

(A)

as
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}
.
Given
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
, if the algorithm
reaches and carries
out the
m^{th}
step without terminating,
let
u_{m}=\left(u_{m}^{(0)},\ldots,u_{m}^{(k)}\right)\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
denote the
approximation found at the
m^{th}
step and

\Sigma_{m}:=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\}\subset\Sigma

denote the points selected
such that at every
z\in\Sigma_{m}
we have

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u_{m}}(z)\leq\varepsilon_{0}

(cf.
HOLGRIM

(C)
and
(D)
).
Then for every
z\in\Sigma
, the quantity

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{m}}(z):=\max_{s\in\{0,\ldots,q\}}\left|\left|\varphi^{(s)}(z)-u_{m}^{(s)}(z)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes s};W)}

satisfies

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{m}}(z)\leq\min\left\{2C~,~2C\max_{h\in\{0,\ldots,q\}}\left\{\dist_{V}(z,\Sigma_{m})^{\gamma-h}\right\}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\dist_{V}(z,\Sigma_{m})}\right\}.

(9.3)

Moreover, whenever
s,t\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
with

s\neq t
we have that

||z_{s}-z_{t}||_{V}>r.

(9.4)

Proof of Lemma

9.1
.

Let
{\cal N},\Lambda,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and fix a choice of
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Let
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}\geq 0
be real numbers
such that
0\leq\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}
.
Assume
V
and
W
are finite dimensional real Banach
spaces, of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively,
with
\Sigma\subset V
a finite subset of cardinality

\Lambda
.
Assume that the tensor products of
V
are all
equipped with admissible norms (cf. Definition

2.1
).
Assume that for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}

the element
f_{i}=\left(f_{i}^{(0)},\ldots,f_{i}^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
is non-zero
and define
{\cal F}:=\left\{f_{i}~:~i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
.
Choose scalars
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such
that, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, we have

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.
Given
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
,
define
\varphi=\left(\varphi^{(0)},\ldots,\varphi^{(k)}\right)\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

and
C>0
by

({\bf I})\quad\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi^{(l)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f^{(l)}_{i}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad({\bf II})\quad C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\left|a_{i}\right|A_{i}>0.

(9.5)

With a view to applying the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
to approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
, for each

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
let
\tilde{a}_{i}:=|a_{i}|

and
\tilde{f}_{i}
be given by
f_{i}
if
a_{i}>0
and

-f_{i}
if
a_{i}<0
.
Observe that for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}

we have that
\left|\left|\tilde{f}_{i}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}=||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}
. Moreover,
we also have that

\tilde{a}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{a}_{{\cal N}}>0
and that

\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\tilde{a}_{i}\tilde{f}_{i}
.
Further, we rescale

\tilde{f}_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}

to have unit
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
norm.
That is (cf.
HOLGRIM

(B)
), for each

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
set

h_{i}:=\tilde{f}_{i}/A_{i}
and

\alpha_{i}:=\tilde{a}_{i}A_{i}
.
A particular consequence is that for every

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
we have

||h_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq 1
.
Additional consequences are that
C
satisfies

C=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\left|a_{i}\right|A_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\tilde{a}_{i}A_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i},

(9.6)

and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, that

\alpha_{i}h_{i}=\tilde{a}_{i}\tilde{f}_{i}=a_{i}f_{i}
.
Thus the expansion for
\varphi
in (
I
) of

(
9.5
)

is equivalent to

\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}h_{i},\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad||\varphi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}||h_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:dist_lemma_new_C}})}}}{{=}}C.

(9.7)

Let
D=D(d,k)
be the constant defined in

(
5.2
)
; that is

D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:D_ab_Q_ijab_def_not_sec}})}}}{{=}}\sum_{s=0}^{k}\beta(d,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:beta_ab_def_not_sec}})}}}{{=}}\sum_{s=0}^{k}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d+s-1\\
s\end{array}\right).

(9.8)

Finally, define

r=r(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)>0
by
(cf.
(
9.2
)
)

r:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0:2C\lambda^{\gamma-q}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\lambda}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}\right\}.

(9.9)

Now consider applying the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
approximate
\varphi
throughout
\Sigma
with the choices
of
M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}
,
the target accuracy threshold in
HOLGRIM

(A)

as
\varepsilon
, the acceptable recombination error in
HOLGRIM

(A)
as
\varepsilon_{0}
, the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
as
q
, the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
as
s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1
, the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)

as
k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
, and the scalars

A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}
as the scaling factors in

HOLGRIM

(A)

Suppose that
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
and that the

HOLGRIM

algorithm reaches and carries out the
m^{th}
step
without terminating.
Let
\Sigma_{m}=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\}\subset\Sigma
denote the points chosen after
the
m^{th}
step is completed.
Then for every
l\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, if we let

\Sigma_{l}:=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\}\subset\Sigma
, we have, recalling

HOLGRIM

(C)
and
(D)
,
that recombination, via Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
,
has found an approximation
u_{l}=\left(u_{l}^{(0)},\ldots,u^{(n)}_{l}\right)\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
of
\varphi

satisfying, for
each
s\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
, that

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u_{l}}(z_{s})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.
That is, given any
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}

and any
s\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
, we have

\left|\left|\varphi^{(j)}(z_{s})-u^{(j)}_{l}(z_{s})\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes j};W)}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

Let
Q_{l}:=1+clD(d,k)
.
Then in the
HOLGRIM Recombination Step
at
step
l
of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm,
recombination is applied, via Lemma 3.1 in
[
LM22-I
]
,
to a system of
Q_{l}
real-valued equations
(cf.
(
6.7
)
).
Therefore recombination returns non-negative coefficients

b_{l,1},\ldots,b_{l,Q_{l}}\geq 0

and indices

e_{l}(1),\ldots,e_{l}(Q_{l})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}

for which

u_{l}=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{l}}b_{l,s}h_{e_{l}(s)}\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u_{l}^{(j)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{l}}b_{l,s}h_{e_{l}(s)}^{(j)}\\
\text{for every }j\in\{0,\ldots,n\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{l}}b_{l,s}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}.

(9.10)

A consequence of
(
9.10
)

is that
