||u_{l}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{l}}b_{l,s}\left|\left|h_{e_{l}(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{l}}b_{l,s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:dist_lemma_ul_expansion}})}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}\alpha_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:dist_lemma_new_C}})}}}{{=}}C.

(9.11)

Consider a point
x\in\Sigma
and let

j\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
be such that

\dist_{V}(x,\Sigma_{l})=||x-z_{j}||_{V}=\dist_{V}(x,z_{j})
.
Consider
h\in\{0,\ldots,q\}
.
By applying Lemma
2.11

to the function
\varphi-u_{l}
,
with the
\Sigma
,
K_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
p
,
\gamma
,
l
, and
k

of that result as
\Sigma
,
2C
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
z_{j}
,
\gamma
,
h
,

k
here respectively, we deduce that
(cf.
(
2.15
)
)

\left|\left|\varphi^{(h)}(x)-u^{(h)}_{l}(x)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes h};W)}\leq\min\left\{2C~,~2C||x-z_{j}||_{V}^{\gamma-h}+\varepsilon_{0}\sum_{s=0}^{k-h}\frac{1}{s!}||x-z_{j}||_{V}^{s}\right\}.

(9.12)

Recalling that
||x-z_{j}||_{V}=\dist_{V}(x,\Sigma_{l})
, it follows
from
(
9.12
)
that

\left|\left|\varphi^{(h)}(x)-u^{(h)}_{l}(x)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes h};W)}\leq\min\left\{2C~,~2C\dist_{V}(x,\Sigma_{l})^{\gamma-h}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\dist_{V}(x,\Sigma_{l})}\right\}.

(9.13)

The arbitrariness of
h\in\{0,\ldots,q\}
and

x\in\Sigma
allows us
to conclude that

(
9.13
)
is
valid for every
h\in\{0,\ldots,q\}
and
every
x\in\Sigma
. As a result we have that

\max_{x\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{l}}(x)\right\}\leq\min\left\{2C~,~2C\max_{h\in\{0,\ldots,q\}}\left\{\dist_{V}(x,\Sigma_{l})^{\gamma-h}\right\}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\dist_{V}(x,\Sigma_{l})}\right\}.

(9.14)

The estimate claimed in

(
9.3
)
is obtained
by taking
l:=m
in

(
9.14
)
.

It remains only to establish the separation
of the points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}
as claimed in

(
9.4
)
.
For this purpose, consider distinct

s,j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}

and without loss of generality assume
s<j
.
By assumption the
HOLGRIM

algorithm does not terminate on
any of the first
m
steps. Therefore

\sigma_{j}:=\argmax_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\ast}_{q}}|\sigma(\varphi-u_{j-1})|
must satisfy that

\left|\sigma_{j}\left(\varphi-u_{j-1}\right)\right|>\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}.

(9.15)

Recall from the
HOLGRIM Extension Step
that

z_{j}\in\Sigma
is taken to be the point for which

\sigma_{j}\in{\cal T}_{z_{j},q}
.
It is then a consequence of Lemma
5.3

that (cf.
(
5.33
)
)

\left|\sigma_{j}\left(\varphi-u_{j-1}\right)\right|\leq\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{j-1}}(z_{j}).

(9.16)

Thus we have that

\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:dist_lemma_sigma_j_lower_bound}})}}}{{<}}\left|\sigma_{j}(\varphi-u_{j-1})\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}({\ref{eq:dist_lemma_pf_sigm_j_eval_ub}})}}}{{\leq}}\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{j-1}}(z_{j}).

(9.17)

Note that
\varphi,u_{j-1}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

and, via

(
9.5
)
and

(
9.11
)
, we have

||\varphi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)},||u_{j-1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq C
.
Further, for any
t\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\}
, we have

\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u_{j-1}}(z_{t})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.
Observe that the definition of
r
here in

(
9.9
)
matches the specification of
the constant
\delta_{0}
in
(
2.12
)
of
the
Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10
for the choices
of the constants
K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
and
l
in that
theorem as
C
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon/cd^{q}
,
\varepsilon_{0}
and
q

here respectively.
Thus, for each
t\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\}
,
we can apply the

Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10

with the choices that
B:=\{z_{t}\}
,

\psi:=\varphi
, and
\phi:=u_{j-1}
to conclude
that for every
x\in{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z_{t},r)~\cap~\Sigma

we have
(cf.
(
2.14
)
)

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{j-1}}(x)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}.

(9.18)

The arbitrariness of
t\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\}

allows us to conclude that

(
9.18
)
is valid
for every
t\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\}
.
Together,
(
9.17
)

and
(
9.18
)
mean
that for every
t\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\}

we must have that

z_{j}\notin{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z_{t},r)\cap\Sigma
.
Since
s\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\}
, we conclude
that
z_{j}\notin{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z_{s},r)\cap\Sigma
,
i.e. that
||z_{j}-z_{s}||_{V}>r

as claimed in
(
9.4
)
.
This completes the proof of Lemma

9.1
.
∎

10
The HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

In this section we establish a convergence result for the

HOLGRIM
algorithm and discuss its consequences.
The following theorem is our main convergence result for the

HOLGRIM
algorithm under the choices that

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD(d,k)},\Lambda\right\}
and,
for every
t\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, that
s_{t}:=1
and

k_{t}:=1
.

Theorem 10.1

(
HOLGRIM Convergence
)
.

Let
{\cal N},\Lambda,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
\gamma>0
with

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that
\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and
fix a choice of
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Let
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0}
be real numbers such that

0\leq\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}
.
Let
V
and
W
be finite dimensional real Banach
spaces, of dimensions
d
and
c
respectively,
with
\Sigma\subset V
finite.
Assume that the tensor products of
V
are all
equipped with admissible norms (cf. Definition

2.1
).
Assume, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, that

f_{i}=\left(f_{i}^{(0)},\ldots,f_{i}^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
is
non-zero and define

{\cal F}:=\left\{f_{i}~:~i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
.
Fix a choice of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that, for
every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, we have

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.
Given
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
,
define
\varphi=\left(\varphi^{(0)},\ldots,\varphi^{(k)}\right)\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

and constants
C,D>0
by

({\bf I})\quad\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i},\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi^{(l)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f^{(l)}_{i}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad({\bf II})\quad C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}|a_{i}|A_{i}>0

(10.1)

and (cf.
(
5.2
)
)

D=D(d,k):=\sum_{s=0}^{k}\beta(d,s)={{\sum}}_{s=0}^{k}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d+s-1\\
s\end{array}\right).

(10.2)

Then there is a positive constant

r=r(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)>0
,

r:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0~:~2C\lambda^{\gamma-q}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\lambda}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{cd^{q}}\right\}

(10.3)

and a non-negative integer

N=N(\Sigma,C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, given by the
r
-packing
number of
\Sigma

N:=\max\left\{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~\exists~z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}\in\Sigma\text{ for which }||z_{a}-z_{b}||_{V}>r\text{ if }a\neq b\right\},

(10.4)

for which the following is true.

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM

algorithm to approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
, with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD},\Lambda\right\}
,

\varepsilon
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\varepsilon_{0}
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,
q
as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and the real numbers

A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}
as the scaling factors
chosen in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.
Suppose that the integer
N
in

(
10.4
)
satisfies

N\leq M
.
Then after at most
N
steps, the algorithm terminates.
That is, there is some integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}

for which the
HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after
completing
m
steps.
Consequently, if we define

Q_{m}:=1+mcD(d,k)
,
there are coefficients

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|A_{e(s)}=C,

(10.5)

and such that
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
defined by

u:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}~\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{(l)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f^{(l)}_{e(s)}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\varepsilon.

(10.6)

Finally, if the coefficients

a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}

corresponding to
\varphi

(cf. (
I
) of
(
10.1
)
)
are all positive (i.e.
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}>0
) then
the coefficients
c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}

corresponding to
u

(cf.
(
10.6
)
) are
all non-negative (i.e.
c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\geq 0
).

Remark 10.2
.

By invoking Lemma

9.1

(cf.
(
9.3
)
) we can
establish pointwise estimates for the sequence of
approximations returned at each step of the
HOLGRIM

algorithm.
That is, suppose
m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that the

HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after step
m
,
and for each
l\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
let
u_{l}\in\Span({\cal F})

denote the approximation found at step
l
via the

HOLGRIM Recombination Step
.
Then Lemma
9.1

yields that, for each
l\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
,
we have, for every point
z\in\Sigma
, that

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u_{l}}(z)\leq\min\left\{2C~,~2C\max_{h\in\{0,\ldots,q\}}\left\{\dist_{V}(z,\Sigma_{l})^{\gamma-h}\right\}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\dist_{V}(z,\Sigma_{l})}\right\}

(10.7)

where
\Sigma_{l}
denotes the subset formed of the
l
points in

\Sigma
that have been selected once step
l
is completed.

Remark 10.3
.

Recall that the
HOLGRIM
algorithm is guaranteed to
terminate after
M
steps. Consequently the
case that
N\leq M
contains all the situations in which
terminating after, at most,
N
steps induces non-trivial
conclusions.
If the integer
N
defined in
(
10.4
)

satisfies
N<M
then Theorem
10.1

guarantees that the
HOLGRIM
algorithm will return
an approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
of
\varphi
that is a linear
combination of
less
than
{\cal N}
of the elements

f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}
, and is within
\varepsilon
of

\varphi
throughout
\Sigma
in the pointwise sense that

\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\varepsilon
.
Consequently, in this case the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
is guaranteed to return an approximation
u
of
\varphi
satisfying

Finite Domain Approximation Conditions

(1)
,
(2)
, and

(3)
from Section
3
.

The integer
N
defined in
(
10.4
)

depends both on the features
{\cal F}
through the constant
C

and the data
\Sigma
.
The constant
C
itself depends only on the values of the
coefficients
a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and the values
of the scaling factors
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}>0
.
The scaling factors depend on the values of the norms

||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)},\ldots,||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}
through the requirement,
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, that

||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq A_{i}
.
No additional constraints are imposed on the collection of
features
{\cal F}
; in particular, we do not assume the existence of
a linear combination of fewer than
{\cal N}
of the features in

{\cal F}
giving a good approximation of
\varphi
throughout

\Sigma
.

The integer
N
defined in
(
10.4
)

is, for the positive number
r>0
determined in

(
10.3
)
,
the
r
-
packing
number of the subset
\Sigma
in

V
as introduced by Kolmogorov
[
Kol56
]
.
Consequently the
r/2
-
covering
number

N_{\cov}(\Sigma,V,r/2)
of
\Sigma
in
V
is an upper bound
for the integer
N
.
Here we use the same notation as used in
[
LM22-I
]
(see,
specifically, Subsection 6.2 in
[
LM22-I
]
).
That is, for any
\rho>0
, we have

N_{\cov}(\Sigma,V,\rho):=\min\left\{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~\exists~z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}\text{ for which we have the inclusion }\Sigma\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{s}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z_{j},\rho)\right\}.

(10.8)

Recall our convention that balls denoted by
{\mathbb{B}}
are taken to be
open whilst those denoted by
{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}
are taken to be closed.
Thus the maximum number of steps that the
HOLGRIM

algorithm can complete before terminating
(cf.
(
10.4
)
) is bounded above by the

r/2
-covering number of
\Sigma
in
V
.
Moreover, this geometric property of the subset
\Sigma
provides
an upper bound on the number of functions from the collection

{\cal F}
appearing in the approximation returned by the

HOLGRIM
algorithm.
Explicit estimates of this covering number provide

worst-case
bounds for the performance of the

HOLGRIM
algorithm.
We illustrate a particular example of this in Remark

10.13
.

Remark 10.4
.

The
HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

fixes
\varepsilon>0
as the accuracy we desire for an approximation,
and then provides an upper bound on the number of functions
from the collection
{\cal F}
that are used by the

HOLGRIM
algorithm to construct an approximation

u
of
\varphi
satisfying, for every
z\in\Sigma
, that

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon
.
But the
HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1
can also be used, for a fixed

n_{0}\in\{2,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, to determine how well the

HOLGRIM
algorithm can approximate
\varphi

throughout
\Sigma
using no more than
n_{0}
of the functions
from
{\cal F}
.

Consider a fixed
n_{0}\in\{2,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
and a
fixed
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.
For
\lambda>0
use the notation

N_{\lambda}:=\max\left\{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~\exists~z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}\in\Sigma\text{ with }||z_{a}-z_{b}||_{V}>\lambda\text{ if }a\neq b\right\}

(10.9)

for the
\lambda
-packing number of
\Sigma
in
V
.
Define
\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(n_{0},c,d,\Sigma,\gamma)>0
and

\beta=\beta(n_{0},c,d,\Sigma,C,\gamma,\varepsilon_{0},q)>0
by

\lambda_{0}:=\inf\left\{\lambda>0~:~N_{\lambda}\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{0}:=cd^{q}\left(2C\lambda_{0}^{\gamma-q}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\lambda_{0}}\right)>0.

(10.10)

A first consequence of
(
10.10
)
is that

0\leq\varepsilon_{0}<\beta_{0}/cd^{q}
.
Hence we can consider applying the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
to approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
with

M:=\min\left\{N_{\lambda_{0}},\Lambda\right\}
,

\beta_{0}
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\varepsilon_{0}
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1
as the shuffle numbers in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers
k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}

in
HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}
as the scaling factors in

HOLGRIM

(A)
.

The definitions of
\lambda_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
in

(
10.10
)
ensure that the positive
constant
r
and the integer
N
arising respectively in

(
10.3
)
and

(
10.4
)
of the

HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

for these choices are given by
\lambda_{0}
and
N_{\lambda_{0}}

respectively.
Consequently the

HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

tells us that this application of the
HOLGRIM

algorithm returns an approximation
u
of
\varphi
that is a
linear combination of at most
n_{0}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
,
and that is within
\beta_{0}
of
\varphi
on
\Sigma
in the
sense that for every
z\in\Sigma
we have

\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\beta_{0}
.

In this way, given any
n_{0}\in\{2,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with

n_{0}\geq 1+cD
,
the relation given in
(
10.10
)

provides a guaranteed accuracy

\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}(n_{0},c,d,\Sigma,C,\gamma,\varepsilon_{0},q)>0

for how well the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can
approximate
\varphi
with the additional constraint that the
approximation is a linear combination of no greater than

n_{0}
of the features in
{\cal F}
. This guarantee ensures both
that there is a linear combination of at most
n_{0}
of the
features in
{\cal F}
that is within
\beta_{0}
of
\varphi

throughout
\Sigma
and that the
HOLGRIM

algorithm will find such a linear combination.
This idea is detailed further in Remarks

10.10
and

10.12
, whilst
an explicit example of such guarantees is provided in
Remark
10.13
.

Remark 10.5
.

One can think of the order level
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}

as determining the order of “derivatives” of
\varphi
that we
seek to approximate throughout
\Sigma
.
For example, if we choose
q:=0
then the

HOLGRIM
algorithm seeks only to approximate
the function
\varphi^{(0)}:\Sigma\to W
in a
pointwise sense throughout
\Sigma
.
And, in this case, Theorem
10.1

establishes conditions under which the

HOLGRIM
algorithm is guaranteed to return
an approximation

u=\left(u^{(0)},\ldots,u^{(k)}\right)\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

for which the function
u^{(0)}:\Sigma\to W

is close to
\varphi^{(0)}
throughout
\Sigma
in the
sense that

\left|\left|\varphi^{(0)}-u^{(0)}\right|\right|_{C^{0}(\Sigma;W)}\leq\varepsilon

(cf.
(
10.6
)
).
On the other hand, if we choose
q:=k
then the

HOLGRIM
algorithm seeks to approximate
the function
\varphi^{(l)}:\Sigma\to{\cal L}(V^{\otimes l};W)

in a pointwise sense throughout
\Sigma

for every
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
And, in this case, Theorem
10.1

establishes conditions under which the

HOLGRIM
algorithm is guaranteed to return
an approximation

u=\left(u^{(0)},\ldots,u^{(k)}\right)\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

for which, for every
l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
the function
u^{(l)}:\Sigma\to{\cal L}(V^{\otimes};W)

is close to
\varphi^{(l)}
throughout
\Sigma
in the
sense that for every
z\in\Sigma
we have

\left|\left|\varphi^{(l)}(z)-u^{(l)}(z)\right|\right|_{{\cal L}(V^{\otimes l};W)}\leq\varepsilon

(cf.
(
10.6
)
).

Remark 10.6
.

The order level
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
in the
HOLGRIM

algorithm determines
the order to which we seek to approximate the function

\varphi=\left(\varphi^{(0)},\ldots,\varphi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
.
A particular consequence of
(
10.3
)

is that a larger value of the order level
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}

leads to a larger upper bound for the maximum number of
steps the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can complete before
terminating.
That is, for fixed values of the parameters
C
,
\gamma
,

\varepsilon
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
the function

q\mapsto r(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)
,
for the
r
defined in
(
10.3
)
,
is decreasing on
\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Hence, again for fixed values of the parameters
C
,
\gamma
,

\varepsilon
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
, the function

q\mapsto N(\Sigma,C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)
,
for the integer
N
defined in

(
10.4
)
,
is increasing on
\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Consequently, seeking only to approximate
\varphi
to
order
q:=0
results in the smallest upper bound
for the maximum number of steps before the

HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates,
whilst seeking to approximate
\varphi
to order

q:=k
results in the largest upper bound.
Larger values of
q
result in the algorithm
finding an approximation of

\varphi
in a stronger sense throughout
\Sigma
, at the
cost of a worse upper bound for the maximum
number of steps the algorithm runs for before
terminating.

Remark 10.7
.

As the regularity parameter
\gamma>0
increases, the integer

N
in
(
10.4
)
giving an upper
bound on the maximum number of steps completed by the

HOLGRIM
algorithm before termination decreases.
To be more precise we adopt the same notation as that of the

HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

and fix the values of the parameters
C
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
, and
fix a choice of
q\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
.
Then consider varying the regularity parameter
\gamma
in the
range
(q,\infty)
; requiring
\gamma>q
ensures that we can
apply the
HOLGRIM
algorithm as in the

HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1
.
The mapping
\gamma\mapsto r(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)

for the constant
r
defined in
(
10.3
)

determines an increasing function on
(q,\infty)
since the
assumption that
\varepsilon\in(0,C)
means that
r\leq 1
.
Consequently the mapping

\gamma\mapsto N(\Sigma,C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},q)
for the
integer
N
defined in
(
10.4
)

determines a decreasing function on
(q,\infty)
.
Hence the data concentration required to guarantee that the

HOLGRIM
algorithm returns an approximation
u
of

\varphi
satisfying that

\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\varepsilon

and that
u
is a linear combination of
strictly less

than
{\cal N}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
is decreasing as the regularity
parameter
\gamma
increases.
That is, a larger value of
\gamma>0
results in a smaller
upper bound
N
on the maximum number of steps completed by the

HOLGRIM
algorithm before it returns an approximation of

\varphi
satisfying

Finite Domain Approximation Conditions

(1)
,
(2)
, and

(3)
from Section
3
.

Remark 10.8
.

The approximation
u
returned by the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
satisfying the accuracy properties guaranteed by Theorem

10.1
additionally satisfies the following

robustness
property.
To illustrate this robustness, assume the notation of Theorem

10.1
and fix a choice of a finite

\xi\in(\varepsilon,\infty)
.
Suppose that
K\geq 1
and that, for every

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, the function

f_{i}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
admits an extension

F_{i}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)
satisfying the norm estimate

||F_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)}\leq K||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}
.

The existence of such an extension is guaranteed by the
Stein-Whitney extension theorem (Theorem 4 in Chapter VI of

[
Ste70
]
. Whilst that result is stated for the case that

V={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
W={\mathbb{R}}
, the proof carries over to our
setting verbatim.
In this result, a method of constructing an extension of
an element
\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

to an element in
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)
is presented.
Moreover, the resulting operator

{\bf E}:{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)\to{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)

mapping an element
\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)

to its extension to an element in
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)
is
established to be a bounded linear operator.

This extension operator is
not
unique; the construction
of an extension of
\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
to
an element in
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)
in the proof of the
Stein-Whitney extension theorem in
[
Ste70
]
involves
making several particular choices of parameters, and varying
each choice results in a different extension.
However, after fixing a particular choice of each parameter,
a verbatim repetition of the
arguments of Stein in Chapter VI of
[
Ste70
]
establishes
the existence
of a constant
K_{{\bf E}}=K_{{\bf E}}(\gamma,d)\geq 1
such that for any

\phi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
we have the norm estimate that

||{\bf E}[\phi]||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)}\leq K_{{\bf E}}||\phi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}
.
Consequently, we could take
K:=K_{{\bf E}}
above.

Recalling that
\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
, it
follows that
\tilde{\varphi}:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}F_{i}
determines
an extension of
\varphi
to an element

\tilde{\varphi}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)
.
It follows that

||\tilde{\varphi}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)}\leq K||\varphi||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq CK
.
Similarly, since
u=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}
, it follows
that
\tilde{u}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}F_{e(s)}
determines an
extension of
u
to an element
\tilde{u}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)
.
It follows, via
(
10.5
)
, that

||\tilde{u}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)}\leq K||u||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq CK
.
Moreover, since
\tilde{\varphi}
and
\tilde{u}
coincide with

\varphi
and
u
respectively throughout
\Sigma
,
the second part of

(
10.6
)
yields that

\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{u}}(z)\right\}=\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\varepsilon.

(10.11)

Fix a choice of
l\in\{0,\ldots,q\}
and
define a constant
r=r(C,K,q,\varepsilon,\xi,l)>0
by

r:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sup\left\{\lambda>0:2CK\lambda^{\gamma-l}+\varepsilon e^{\lambda}\leq\xi\right\}&\mbox{if }q=k\\
\sup\left\{\lambda>0:2eCK\lambda^{q+1-l}+\varepsilon e^{\lambda}\leq\xi\right\}&\mbox{if }q<k.\end{array}\right.

(10.12)

Set
\Omega:=\Sigma_{r}:=\left\{v\in V:\exists~u\in\Sigma\text{ with }||u-v||_{V}\leq r\right\}

to be the
r
-fattening of
\Sigma
in
V
.
We claim that the extensions
\tilde{\varphi}
and
\tilde{u}
of

\varphi
and
u
respectively remain close throughout

\Omega
in the sense that

\sup_{z\in\Omega}\left\{\Lambda^{l}_{\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{u}}(z)\right\}\leq\xi.

(10.13)

We first verify

(
10.13
)

in the case that
q=k
.
In this case, an examination of
r
as defined in

(
10.12
)
reveals that it coincides
with the specification of the constant
\delta_{0}
in

(
2.12
)
of the

Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10
for the choices
of
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
K_{0}
,
\gamma
, and
l
there as

\xi
,
\varepsilon
,
CK
,
\gamma
, and
l
here respectively.
Therefore
(
10.11
)

provides the required hypothesis to
appeal to the

Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10

with
B:=\Sigma
,
\psi:=\tilde{\varphi}
, and
\phi:=\tilde{u}
.
By applying this result, we conclude, for every
z\in\Omega
, that

\Lambda^{l}_{\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{u}}(z)\leq\xi
as claimed in

(
10.13
)
.

We now consider the case that
q<k
so that
k\geq 1
and

q\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
.
Recall the notation that for any

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
and any
n\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
that

\phi_{[n]}:=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(n)}\right)
.
We use the analogous notation for elements in

{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)
.

Since
q+1\leq k<\gamma
we can
invoke Lemma 6.1 in
[
LM24
]
to deduce that

\tilde{\varphi}_{[q]},\tilde{u}_{[q]}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(q+1,\Sigma,W)

with

||\tilde{\varphi}_{[q]}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(q+1,\Sigma,W)}\leq e||\tilde{\varphi}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq eCK
and

||\tilde{u}_{[q]}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(q+1,\Sigma,W)}\leq e||\tilde{u}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)}\leq eCK
.
An examination of
r
as defined in

(
10.12
)
reveals that it coincides
with the specification of the constant
\delta_{0}
in

(
2.12
)
of the

Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10
for the choices
of
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
K_{0}
,
\gamma
, and
l
there as

\xi
,
\varepsilon
,
eCK
,
q+1
, and
l
here respectively.
Therefore
(
10.11
)

provides the required hypothesis to appeal
to the
Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10

with
B:=\Sigma
,
\psi:=\tilde{\varphi}_{[q]}
,
and
\phi:=\tilde{u}_{[q]}
.
By doing so, we conclude, for every
z\in\Omega
, that

\Lambda^{l}_{\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{u}}(z)\leq\xi

as claimed in

(
10.13
)
.

Thus the extension
\tilde{u}
is a linear combination of

Q_{m}
of the functions
F_{1},\ldots,F_{{\cal N}}
that is
within
\xi
of the extension
\tilde{\varphi}
throughout

\Omega
in the pointwise sense that

\sup_{z\in\Omega}\left\{\Lambda^{l}_{\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{u}}(z)\right\}\leq\xi
.
Loosely speaking, the approximation on
\Sigma

found by the
HOLGRIM
algorithm remains a good
approximation on points in
V
that are not too far away from

\Sigma
.
To be more precise, provided we are given, for each

i\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
, an extension
F_{i}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,V,W)

of
f_{i}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Sigma,W)
, the weights

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and the indices

e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
returned by
the
HOLGRIM
algorithm, determining a good approximation
of
\varphi
throughout
\Sigma
via the linear combination

u=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}
of the functions

f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}
, additionally determine a good
approximation of the extension
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\varphi

throughout
\Omega
via the linear combination

\tilde{u}=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}F_{e(s)}
of the extensions

F_{1},\ldots,F_{{\cal N}}
of the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}
.

Remark 10.9
.

Following the strategy outlined in Section

4
, we may combine the
theoretical guarantees for the
HOLGRIM

algorithm provided by Theorem
10.1
with
the
Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10
(cf. Theorem 3.11 in
[
LM24
]
)
to extend the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to the setting
in which the
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma)
functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}

are defined throughout an arbitrary non-empty compact subset

\Omega\subset V
rather than a finite subset.
To make this precise, let
{\cal N}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and
suppose that
\Omega\subset V
is a fixed
non-empty compact subset with

f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
.
As in Theorem
10.1
we consider

\varphi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
for non-zero coefficients

a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let

C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}|a_{i}|||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}>0
,
and take
D=D(d,k)
to be as defined in

(
10.2
)
.
We now illustrate how the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can
be used to obtain an approximation of
\varphi
throughout
the compact subset
\Omega\subset V
.

Fix choices of
\varepsilon\in(0,C)
and
0\leq\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon/2cd^{k}

and
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Define a positive constant

\delta=\delta(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,c,d,q)>0
by

\delta:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0~:~2C\lambda^{\gamma-q}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e^{\lambda}\leq\varepsilon\right\}.

(10.14)

Next we define an integer

\Lambda=\Lambda(\Omega,C,\gamma,\varepsilon,c,d,q)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}

by

\Lambda:=\min\left\{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}~:~\exists~z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}\in\Omega\text{ such that }\Omega\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{s}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z_{j},\delta)\right\}.

(10.15)

The compactness of the subset
\Omega\subset V
ensures
that the integer
\Lambda
defined in

(
10.15
)
is finite.

Choose a finite subset
\Sigma=\left\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{\Lambda}\right\}\subset\Omega
of cardinality
\Lambda
for which

\Omega\subset\cup_{s=1}^{\Lambda}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z_{s},\delta)
.
With the finite subset
\Sigma
fixed, define a positive
constant
r=r(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},c,d)>0
by
(cf.
(
10.3
)
)

r:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0~:~2C\lambda^{\gamma-k}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\lambda}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2cd^{k}}\right\},

(10.16)

and define an integer

N=N(\Sigma,C,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},c,d)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by
(cf.
(
10.4
)
)

N:=\max\left\{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}~:~\exists~z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}\in\Sigma\text{ for which }||z_{a}-z_{b}||_{V}>r\text{ if }a\neq b\right\}.

(10.17)

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM

algorithm to approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
, with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD},\Lambda\right\}
,

\varepsilon/2
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\varepsilon_{0}
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,
k
as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}
as the
scaling factors chosen in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.

If the integer
N
defined in

(
10.17
)
satisfies that

N\leq M
, then
Theorem
10.1
guarantees that
there is some integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}

for which the
HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after
completing
m
steps. Thus, if
Q_{m}:=1+mcD(d,k)
,
there are coefficients

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with
(cf.
(
10.5
)
)

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|\left|\left|f_{e(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}=C,

(10.18)

and such that
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by
(cf.
(
10.6
)
)

u:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}~\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{(l)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f^{(l)}_{e(s)}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

(10.19)

Observe that the condition

(
10.18
)
means that

||u||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}\leq C
.
Recall that the finite subset
\Sigma\subset\Omega
was
chosen to be a
\delta
-cover of
\Omega
in the sense that

\Omega\subset\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z,\delta)
.
Therefore the definition of
\delta
in

(
10.14
)
and
the estimates in

(
10.19
)
provide
the hypotheses required to appeal to the

Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10
with the

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
l
,
\Sigma
,
B
,

\psi
, and
\phi
of that result as

C
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon/2
,
q
,
\Omega
,
\Sigma
,

\varphi
, and
u
here respectively.
Indeed the definition of
\delta
here in

(
10.14
)
coincides with
the definition of
\delta_{0}
in
(
2.12
)

for the choices specified above.
Thus we may invoke the implication

(
2.14
)
to conclude that

\sup_{z\in\Omega}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\varepsilon.

(10.20)

Consequently the
HOLGRIM
algorithm has returned an
approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)

of
\varphi
that has the sparsity properties as outlined in
Remark
10.3
and is
within
\varepsilon
of
\varphi
throughout
\Omega

in the pointwise sense that, for every point
z\in\Omega
,
we have
\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\leq\varepsilon
.
That is, in this case the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
is guaranteed to return an approximation
u
of
\varphi
satisfying

Compact Domain Approximation Conditions

(1)
,
(2)
, and

(3)
from Section

4
.

Remark 10.10
.

Following the approach outlined in Remark

10.4
, we illustrate the
theoretical guarantees available for the
HOLGRIM

algorithm when used as in Remark

10.9

but with an additional restriction on the maximum
number of functions from
{\cal F}
that may be used to construct
the approximation
u
.

To make this precise, let
{\cal N}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
, and
suppose that
\Omega\subset V
is a fixed
non-empty compact subset with

f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
.
As in Theorem
10.1
we consider

\varphi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
for non-zero coefficients

a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let

C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}|a_{i}|||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}>0
,
and take
D=D(d,k)
to be as defined in

(
10.2
)
.
Fix a choice of
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
and, assuming that

{\cal N}>1+cD
, fix
a choice of integer
n_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
such that

n_{0}\geq 1+cD
.
We consider the guarantees available for the pointwise quantity

\sup_{z\in\Omega}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}

for the approximation returned by the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
with the restriction that
u
is a linear combination of at most

n_{0}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
.

For this purpose we fix
\theta\geq 0
and define

\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(n_{0},c,d,\gamma)>0
and

\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}(n_{0},C,c,d,\gamma,\theta)>0
by

\lambda_{0}:=\inf\left\{t>0~:~N_{\pack}(\Omega,V,t)\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD}\right\}>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{0}:=2cd^{k}\left(2C\lambda_{0}^{\gamma-k}+\theta e^{\lambda_{0}}\right)>0.

(10.21)

With the value of
\beta_{0}>0
fixed, define a positive
constant
\delta=\delta(n_{0},C,c,d,\gamma,\theta,q)>0
and an integer

\Lambda=\Lambda(\Omega,n_{0},C,c,d,\gamma,\theta,q)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by

\delta:=\sup\left\{t>0~:~2Ct^{\gamma-q}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}e^{t}\leq\beta_{0}\right\}>0\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda:=N_{\cov}(\Omega,V,\delta).

(10.22)

Since
\Omega\subset V
is compact, we have that

\Lambda
defined in

(
10.22
)

is finite. Choose a subset

\Sigma=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{\Lambda}\}\subset\Omega\subset V

for which
\Omega\subset\cup_{s=1}^{\Lambda}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(p_{s},\delta)
.
Moreover, since

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,V,\lambda_{0})\leq N_{\pack}(\Omega,V,\lambda_{0})
,
we have via

(
10.21
)
that

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,V,\lambda_{0})\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD}
.
Furthermore, it additionally follows from

(
10.21
)

that
0\leq\theta<\beta_{0}/2cd^{k}
.

Therefore we may apply the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD},\Lambda\right\}
,

\beta_{0}/2
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\theta
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,
k
as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}
as the
scaling factors chosen in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.

Observe that
\lambda_{0}
defined in

(
10.52
)

coincides with the positive constant
r>0
arising in

(
10.3
)
in the

HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

for the constants
C
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
, and
q
there
as
C
,
\gamma
,
\beta_{0}/2
,
\theta
and
k
here. Thus, since

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,V,\lambda_{0})\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD}
,
the
HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

tells us that there is an integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}

for which the
HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after
completing
m
steps. Thus, if
Q_{m}:=1+mcD(d,k)
,
there are coefficients

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with
(cf.
(
10.5
)
)

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|\left|\left|f_{e(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}=C,

(10.23)

and such that
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by
(cf.
(
10.6
)
)

u:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}~\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{(l)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f^{(l)}_{e(s)}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}.

(10.24)

The condition

(
10.23
)

means that
||u||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}\leq C
.
Recall that the finite subset
\Sigma\subset\Omega
was
chosen to be a
\delta
-cover of
\Omega
in the sense that

\Omega\subset\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z,\delta)
.
Therefore the definition of
\delta
in

(
10.53
)
and
the estimates in

(
10.56
)
provide
the hypotheses required to appeal to the

Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10
with the

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
l
,
\Sigma
,
B
,

\psi
, and
\phi
of that result as

C
,
\gamma
,
\beta_{0}
,
\beta_{0}/2
,
q
,
\Omega
,
\Sigma
,

\varphi
, and
u
here respectively.
Indeed the definition of
\delta
here in

(
10.53
)

coincides with
the definition of
\delta_{0}
in
(
2.12
)

for the choices specified above.
Thus we may invoke the implication

(
2.14
)
to conclude that

\sup_{z\in\Omega}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\beta_{0}.

(10.25)

Moreover, we have that
Q_{m}\leq 1+cMD\leq n_{0}
so that

u
is guaranteed to be a linear combination of no greater than

n_{0}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
.
Thus given an integer
n_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}

satisfying
n_{0}\geq 1+cD(d,k)
, we are
guaranteed that the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can find
an approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})
of
\varphi
that is a linear
combination of no more than
n_{0}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
,
and is within
\beta_{0}
, for
\beta_{0}
defined in

(
10.21
)
,
of
\varphi
throughout
\Omega
in the pointwise sense detailed
in
(
10.25
)
.

Remark 10.11
.

Following the strategy outlined in Section

4
, we may combine the
theoretical guarantees for the
HOLGRIM

algorithm provided by Theorem
10.1
with
the
Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
(cf. Theorem 3.1 in
[
LM24
]
)
to extend the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to the setting in which
the
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma)
functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}

are defined throughout an arbitrary non-empty compact subset

\Omega\subset V
rather than a finite subset, and strengthen
the sense in which the returned approximation
u
approximates

\varphi
throughout
\Omega
to a
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta)
sense for a
given
\eta\in(0,\gamma)
.

To make this precise, let
{\cal N}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
,

\eta\in(0,\gamma)
with
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
such that

\eta\in(q,q+1]
, and
suppose that
\Omega\subset V
is a fixed
non-empty compact subset with

f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
.
As in Theorem
10.1
we consider

\varphi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
for non-zero coefficients

a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Making the choices

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}

in
(
10.1
)
, take

C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}|a_{i}|||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}>0
,
and take
D=D(d,k)
to be as defined in

(
10.2
)
.
We now illustrate how the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can
be used to obtain an approximation
u
of
\varphi
throughout
the compact subset
\Omega\subset V
in the sense that
the
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,\Omega,W)
norm of the difference
\varphi-u

is small.

Fix a choice of
\varepsilon\in(0,C)
.
Retrieve the constants

\delta=\delta(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)>0
and

\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)>0

arising as
\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
respectively in the

Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9

for the constants
K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there
as
C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
here respectively.
Note we are not actually applying Theorem
2.9
,
but simply retrieving constants in preparation for its future
application.
Subsequently define an integer

\Lambda=\Lambda(\Omega,C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}

as the
\delta
-covering number of
\Omega
in
V
; that is

\Lambda:=\min\left\{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}~:~\exists~z_{1},\ldots,z_{a}\in\Omega\text{ for which }\Omega\subset\bigcup_{s=1}^{a}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z_{s},\delta)\right\}.

(10.26)

The subset
\Omega\subset V
being compact ensures that

\Lambda
defined in

(
10.26
)

is finite. Choose

\Sigma=\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{\Lambda}\right\}\subset\Omega\subset V
such that

\Omega\subset\cup_{s=1}^{\Lambda}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(p_{s},\delta)
.

With the finite subset
\Sigma\subset\Omega\subset V
fixed,
we now define
r=r(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)>0
by

r:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0~:~2C\lambda^{\gamma-k}+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2cd^{k}}e^{\lambda}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}\right\}.

(10.27)

Subsequently define an integer

N=N(\Sigma,C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by

N:=\max\left\{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~\exists~z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}\in\Sigma\text{ for which }||z_{a}-z_{b}||_{V}>r\text{ if }a\neq b\right\}.

(10.28)

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM

algorithm to approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
, with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{cD},\Lambda\right\}
,

\varepsilon_{0}
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\varepsilon_{0}/2cd^{k}
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,
k
as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}
as the
scaling factors chosen in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.

If the integer
N
defined in

(
10.17
)
satisfies that

N\leq M
, then
Theorem
10.1
guarantees that
there is some integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}

for which the
HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after
completing
m
steps. Thus, if
Q_{m}:=1+mcD(d,k)
,
there are coefficients

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with
(cf.
(
10.5
)
)

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|\left|\left|f_{e(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}=C,

(10.29)

and such that
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by
(cf.
(
10.6
)
)

u:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}~\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{(l)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f^{(l)}_{e(s)}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

(10.30)

Observe that the condition

(
10.29
)
means that

||u||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}\leq C
.
Recall that the finite subset
\Sigma\subset\Omega
was
chosen to be a
\delta
-cover of
\Omega
in the sense that

\Omega\subset\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z,\delta)
.
Therefore, recalling how the constants
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0}
here
were retrieved from the
Lipschtz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
, we have the required hypotheses
in order to appeal to the
Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
for the constants

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there as

C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
here respectively,
the subsets
B
and
\Sigma
there are
\Sigma
and
\Omega

here respectively, and the functions
\psi
and
\phi
there
as
\varphi
and
u
here respectively.
By doing so, we may conclude via the implication

(
2.11
)
that

\left|\left|\varphi_{[q]}-u_{[q]}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,\Omega,W)}\leq\varepsilon

(10.31)

where we use the notation that if

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
then

\phi_{[q]}:=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(q)}\right)
.
Consequently the
HOLGRIM
algorithm has returned an
approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)

of
\varphi
that has the sparsity properties as outlined in
Remark
10.3
and is
within
\varepsilon
of
\varphi
in the
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,\Omega,W)
norm
sense detailed in

(
10.31
)
.
That is, in this case the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
is guaranteed to return an approximation
u
of
\varphi
satisfying

Compact Domain Lipschitz Approximation Conditions

(1)
,

(2)
, and

(3)
from Section

4
.

Remark 10.12
.

Following the approach outlined in Remark

10.4
, we illustrate the
theoretical guarantees available for the
HOLGRIM

algorithm when used as in Remark

10.11

but with an additional restriction on the maximum
number of functions from
{\cal F}
that may be used to construct
the approximation
u
.

To make this precise, let
{\cal N}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

\gamma>0
with
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that

\gamma\in(k,k+1]
,
\eta\in(0,\gamma)
with

q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
such that

\eta\in(q,q+1]
, and
suppose that
\Omega\subset V
is a fixed
non-empty compact subset with

f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
.
As in Theorem
10.1
we consider

\varphi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
for non-zero coefficients

a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}|a_{i}|||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}>0
,
and take
D=D(d,k)
to be as defined in

(
10.2
)
.
Assuming that
{\cal N}>1+cD
, fix
a choice of integer
n_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
such that

n_{0}\geq 1+cD
.
We consider the guarantees available for the quantity

||\varphi_{[q]}-u_{[q]}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,\Omega,W)}

for the approximation returned by the
HOLGRIM
algorithm
with the restriction that
u
is a linear combination of at most

n_{0}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
. Here we use the notation that if

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
then

\phi_{[q]}:=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(q)}\right)
.
We will see that the stronger sense in which
u

approximates
\varphi
throughout
\Omega
comes at a cost of
less explicit constants.

Fix
\theta\geq 0
and define

\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(n_{0},c,d,\gamma)>0
and

\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}(n_{0},C,c,d,\gamma,\theta)>0
by

\lambda_{0}:=\inf\left\{t>0~:~N_{\pack}(\Omega,V,t)\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD}\right\}>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{0}:=cd^{k}\left(2C\lambda_{0}^{\gamma-k}+\theta e^{\lambda_{0}}\right)>0.

(10.32)

It follows from

(
10.32
)

that
0\leq\theta<\beta_{0}/cd^{k}
.
With the value of
\beta_{0}>0
fixed, determine a positive
constant
\xi=\xi(n_{0},C,c,d,\gamma,\eta,\theta)>0
by

\xi:=\inf\left\{\varepsilon>0~:~\beta_{0}\leq\varepsilon_{0}(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)\right\}

(10.33)

where, given
\varepsilon>0
,

\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)>0
denotes the constant

\varepsilon_{0}
retrieved from the
Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
for the choices of the constants

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there as

C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
here respectively.
After the value of
\xi>0
is fixed, retrieve the constant

\delta=\delta(n_{0},C,c,d,\gamma,\eta,\theta)>0
arising as the constant

\delta_{0}
in the
Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
for the choices of the constants

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there as

C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\xi
here respectively.
Finally, define an integer

\Lambda=\Lambda(\Omega,n_{0},C,c,d,\gamma,\theta,q)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by

\Lambda:=N_{\cov}(\Omega,V,\delta).

(10.34)

Since the subset
\Omega\subset V
is compact, the integer

\Lambda
defined in

(
10.60
)

is finite. Choose a subset

\Sigma=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{\Lambda}\}\subset\Omega

for which
\Omega\subset\cup_{s=1}^{\Lambda}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(p_{s},\delta)
.
Moreover, since
N_{\pack}(\Sigma,V,\lambda_{0})\leq N_{\pack}(\Omega,V,\lambda_{0})
,

(
10.32
)

ensures that

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,V,\lambda_{0})\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD}
.

Apply the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD},\Lambda\right\}
,

\beta_{0}
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\theta
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,
k
as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}
as the
scaling factors chosen in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.

Observe that
\lambda_{0}
defined in

(
10.32
)

coincides with the positive constant
r>0
arising in

(
10.3
)
in the

HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

for the constants
C
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
, and
q
there
as
C
,
\gamma
,
\beta_{0}
,
\theta
and
k
here. Thus, since

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,V,\lambda_{0})\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{cD}
,
the
HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1

tells us that there is an integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}

for which the
HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after
completing
m
steps. Thus, if
Q_{m}:=1+mcD(d,k)
,
there are coefficients

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with
(cf.
(
10.5
)
)

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|\left|\left|f_{e(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}=C,

(10.35)

and such that
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
defined by
(cf.
(
10.6
)
)

u:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}~\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{(l)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f^{(l)}_{e(s)}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\beta_{0}.

(10.36)

The condition

(
10.35
)

means that
||u||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}\leq C
.
Recall that the finite subset
\Sigma\subset\Omega
was
chosen to be a
\delta
-cover of
\Omega
in the sense that

\Omega\subset\cup_{z\in\Sigma}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}_{V}(z,\delta)
.
Further recall that
\delta
is the constant
\delta_{0}
arising
in the
Lipschtz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
for the choices of the constants

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there as

C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\xi
here.
Finally, recall that
\xi>0
was chosen in

(
10.59
)

so that
\beta_{0}\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
where
\varepsilon_{0}
is the constant

\varepsilon_{0}
arising in the
Lipschtz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
for the choices of the constants

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there as

C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\xi
here.

Therefore, we have the required hypotheses to appeal to the

Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
for the constants

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there as

C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\xi
here respectively,
the subsets
B
and
\Sigma
there are
\Sigma
and
\Omega

here respectively, and the functions
\psi
and
\phi
there
as
\varphi
and
u
here respectively.
By doing so, we may conclude via the implication

(
2.11
)
that

\left|\left|\varphi_{[q]}-u_{[q]}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,\Omega,W)}\leq\xi

(10.37)

where we use the notation that if

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)
then

\phi_{[q]}:=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(q)}\right)
.

Moreover, we have that
Q_{m}\leq 1+cMD\leq n_{0}
so that

u
is guaranteed to be a linear combination of no greater than

n_{0}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
.
Thus given an integer
n_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with

n_{0}\geq 1+cD(d,k)
, we are
guaranteed that the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can find
an approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})
of
\varphi
that is a linear
combination of no more than
n_{0}
of the functions in
{\cal F}
,
and is within
\xi
, for
\xi
defined in

(
10.33
)
,
of
\varphi
throughout
\Omega
in the
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,\Omega,W)

norm sense detailed
in
(
10.37
)
.

Remark 10.13
.

We now provide an illustrative example of several of the
previous remarks regarding the
HOLGRIM Convergence Theorem

10.1
.
For this purpose consider a fixed
\gamma>0
with

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
such that
\gamma\in(k,k+1]
,
a fixed
\eta\in(0,\gamma)
with
q\in\{0,\ldots,k\}

such that
\eta\in(q,q+1]
, a fixed
\varepsilon>0
, and a
fixed
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.
Take
V:={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
equipped with its usual Euclidean norm

||\cdot||_{2}
, take
\Omega:=[0,1]^{d}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
to be
the unit cube in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and take
W:={\mathbb{R}}
.
The norm
||\cdot||_{2}
is induced by the standard Euclidean
dot product
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Equip the tensor powers of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with admissible norms in the
sense of Definition
2.1
by
extending the inner product
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}

to the tensor powers, and subsequently taking the norm induced
by the resulting inner product on each tensor power
(see, for example, Section 2 in
[
LM24
]
for details of this
construction).
Introduce the notation, for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
r>0
, that

{\mathbb{B}}^{d}(x,r):=\left\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:||x-y||_{2}<r\right\}
.

Let
\rho\in(0,1)
. Then we have that

N_{\cov}\left([0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\rho\right)\leq N_{\pack}\left([0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\rho\right)\leq\frac{2^{d}}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\rho}\right)^{d}

(10.38)

where
\omega_{d}
denotes the Euclidean volume of the unit-ball

{\mathbb{B}}^{d}(0,1)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
The first inequality in
(
10.38
)

is a consequence of the fact that the

\rho
-covering number of a set is always no greater than
its
\rho
-packing number.
The second inequality in
(
10.38
)

is a consequence of a volume comparison argument,
the details of which may be found, for example, in
Remark 4.4 in
[
LM24
]
.

Fix
{\cal N}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and consider a collection

{\cal F}=\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{{\cal N}}\}\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})

of non-zero functions in
{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
.
Fix a choice of non-zero coefficients

a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\cal N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and define

\varphi\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
by

\varphi:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}a_{i}f_{i}
.
Set
C:=\sum_{i=1}^{{\cal N}}|a_{i}|||f_{i}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})}>0

and
D=D(d,k)
to be the constant defined in

(
10.2
)
.

We first consider the use of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
find an approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})
of
\varphi

satisfying that
\sup_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(x)\right\}\leq\varepsilon
by following
the strategy detailed in Remark

10.9
.
Define a positive constant

\delta=\delta(C,\gamma,\varepsilon,q)>0
and an integer

\Lambda=\Lambda(C,d,\gamma,\varepsilon,q)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}

by (cf.
(
10.14
)
)

\delta:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0~:~2C\lambda^{\gamma-q}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e^{\lambda}\leq\varepsilon\right\}>0\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda:=\max\left\{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~m\leq\frac{2^{d}}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{d}\right\}.

(10.39)

A consequence of
(
10.38
)
and

(
10.39
)
is that

N_{\cov}([0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\delta)\leq\Lambda
, and so
we may choose a subset

\Sigma=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{\Lambda}\}\subset[0,1]^{d}
for which

[0,1]^{d}\subset\cup_{s=1}^{\Lambda}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}^{d}(p_{s},\delta)
.

Now fix a choice of
\varepsilon_{0}\in[0,\varepsilon/2d^{k})
and define
a positive constant
r=r(C,d,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0})>0
and an integer

N=N(C,d,\gamma,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by
(cf.
(
10.16
)
)

r:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0:2C\lambda^{\gamma-k}+\varepsilon_{0}e^{\lambda}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2d^{k}}\right\}>0\quad\text{and}\quad N:=\max\left\{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~m\leq\frac{2^{d}}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{r}\right)^{d}\right\}.

(10.40)

It is then a consequence of
(
10.38
)

and
(
10.40
)
that

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}}^{d},r)\leq N
.

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM

algorithm to approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
, with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{D},\Lambda\right\}
,

\varepsilon/2
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\varepsilon_{0}
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,
k
as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}
as the
scaling factors chosen in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.

If the integer
N
defined in

(
10.40
)
satisfies that

N\leq M
, then
Theorem
10.1
guarantees that
there is some integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}

for which the
HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after
completing
m
steps. Thus, if
Q_{m}:=1+mD(d,k)
,
there are coefficients

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with
(cf.
(
10.5
)
)

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|\left|\left|f_{e(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})}=C,

(10.41)

and such that
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
defined by
(cf.
(
10.6
)
)

u:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}~\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{(l)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f^{(l)}_{e(s)}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{z\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(z)\right\}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

(10.42)

Observe that the condition

(
10.41
)

means that
||u||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})}\leq C
.
Recall that the finite subset
\Sigma\subset[0,1]^{d}
was
chosen to be a
\delta
-cover of
[0,1]^{d}
in the sense that

[0,1]^{d}\subset\cup_{x\in\Sigma}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}^{d}(x,\delta)
.
Therefore the definition of
\delta
in

(
10.39
)
and
the estimates in

(
10.42
)
provide
the hypotheses required to appeal to the

Pointwise Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.10
with the

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
l
,
\Sigma
,
B
,

\psi
, and
\phi
of that result as

C
,
\gamma
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon/2
,
q
,
[0,1]^{d}
,
\Sigma
,

\varphi
, and
u
here respectively.
Indeed the definition of
\delta
here in

(
10.39
)

coincides with
the definition of
\delta_{0}
in
(
2.12
)

for the choices specified above.
Thus we may invoke the implication

(
2.14
)
to conclude that

\sup_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(x)\right\}\leq\varepsilon.

(10.43)

Consequently the
HOLGRIM
algorithm has returned an
approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})

of
\varphi
that is
within
\varepsilon
of
\varphi
throughout
[0,1]^{d}

in the pointwise sense that, for every point
x\in[0,1]^{d}
,
we have
\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(x)\leq\varepsilon
.
Moreover,
u
is a linear combination of
Q_{m}=1+mD(d,k)

of the
{\cal N}
functions in
{\cal F}
.
Thus, if we are in the setting that
D\Lambda\leq{\cal N}-1
,
then
(
10.39
)

yields the worst-case upper bound of

Q_{m}\leq 1+\Lambda D\leq 1+D+\frac{2^{d}D}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{d}

(10.44)

for the number of functions from
{\cal N}
that are used to
construct the approximation
u
returned by the

HOLGRIM
algorithm.

We next consider the use of the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
find an approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})
of
\varphi

satisfying that

||\varphi_{[q]}-u_{[q]}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})}\leq\varepsilon

by following the strategy detailed in Remark

10.11
.
Retrieve the constants

\delta=\delta(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)>0
and

\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)>0

arising as
\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
respectively in the

Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9

for the constants
K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there
as
C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
here respectively.
Note we are not actually applying Theorem
2.9
,
but simply retrieving constants in preparation for its future
application.
Subsequently define an integer

\Lambda=\Lambda(C,d,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by

\Lambda:=\max\left\{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}~:~m\leq\frac{2^{d}}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{d}\right\}.

(10.45)

A consequence of
(
10.38
)
and

(
10.45
)
is
that
N_{\cov}([0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\delta)\leq\Lambda
, and so
we may choose a subset

\Sigma=\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{\Lambda}\right\}\subset[0,1]^{d}
for which

[0,1]^{d}\subset\cup_{s=1}^{\Lambda}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}^{d}(p_{s},\delta)
.

With the finite subset
\Sigma\subset[0,1]^{d}
fixed,
we now first define
r=r(C,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)>0
by

r:=\sup\left\{\lambda>0~:~2C\lambda^{\gamma-k}+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2cd^{k}}e^{\lambda}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{cd^{k}}\right\}>0

(10.46)

and then an integer

N=N(C,d,\gamma,\eta,\varepsilon)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by

N:=\max\left\{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~m\leq\frac{2^{d}}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{r}\right)^{d}\right\}.

(10.47)

It is then a consequence of
(
10.38
)
and

(
10.47
)
that

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}}^{d},r)\leq N
.

Consider applying the
HOLGRIM

algorithm to approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
, with

M:=\min\left\{\frac{{\cal N}-1}{D},\Lambda\right\}
,

\varepsilon_{0}
as the target accuracy in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

\varepsilon_{0}/2cd^{k}
as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,
k
as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}
as the
scaling factors chosen in
HOLGRIM

(A)
.

If the integer
N
defined in

(
10.47
)
satisfies that

N\leq M
, then
Theorem
10.1
guarantees that
there is some integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}

for which the
HOLGRIM
algorithm terminates after
completing
m
steps. Thus, if
Q_{m}:=1+mD(d,k)
,
there are coefficients

c_{1},\ldots,c_{Q_{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
indices
e(1),\ldots,e(Q_{m})\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with
(cf.
(
10.5
)
)

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|\left|\left|f_{e(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})}=C,

(10.48)

and such that
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
defined by
(cf.
(
10.6
)
)

u:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f_{e(s)}~\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{(l)}:=\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}c_{s}f^{(l)}_{e(s)}\\
\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{x\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(x)\right\}\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

(10.49)

Observe that the condition

(
10.48
)

means that

||u||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})}\leq C
.
Recall that the finite subset
\Sigma\subset[0,1]^{d}
was
chosen to be a
\delta
-cover of
[0,1]^{d}
in the sense that

[0,1]^{d}\subset\cup_{x\in\Sigma}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}^{d}(x,\delta)
.
Therefore, recalling how the constants
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0}
here
were retrieved from the
Lipschtz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
, we have the required hypotheses
in order to appeal to the
Lipschitz Sandwich Theorem

2.9
for the constants

K_{0}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
there as

C
,
\gamma
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
here respectively,
the subsets
B
and
\Sigma
there are
\Sigma
and
[0,1]^{d}

here respectively, and the functions
\psi
and
\phi
there
as
\varphi
and
u
here respectively.
By doing so, we may conclude via the implication

(
2.11
)
that

\left|\left|\varphi_{[q]}-u_{[q]}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,\Omega,W)}\leq\varepsilon

(10.50)

where we use the notation that if

\phi=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(k)}\right)\in{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
then

\phi_{[q]}:=\left(\phi^{(0)},\ldots,\phi^{(q)}\right)
.
Consequently the
HOLGRIM
algorithm has returned an
approximation
u\in\Span({\cal F})\subset{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})

of
\varphi
that is within
\varepsilon
of
\varphi
in the

{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
norm sense detailed in

(
10.50
)
.
Moreover,
u
is a linear combination of
Q_{m}=1+mD(d,k)

of the
{\cal N}
functions in
{\cal F}
.
Thus, if we are in the setting that
D\Lambda\leq{\cal N}-1
,
then
(
10.45
)

yields the worst-case upper bound of

Q_{m}\leq 1+\Lambda D\leq 1+D+\frac{2^{d}D}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{d}

(10.51)

for the number of functions from
{\cal N}
that are used to
construct the approximation
u
returned by the

HOLGRIM
algorithm.

Assume now that
{\cal N}>1+2^{d}D/\omega_{d}
.
We fix an integer
n_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,{\cal N}\}
with

n_{0}\geq 1+2^{d}D/\omega_{d}
and
consider how well the
HOLGRIM
algorithm can
approximate
\varphi
throughout
[0,1]^{d}
using at most

n_{0}
functions from
{\cal F}
(cf. Remark

10.4
).
We first consider the guarantees available for the pointwise
quantity
\sup_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}\left\{\Lambda^{q}_{\varphi-u}(x)\right\}
for the approximation
u
returned by the

HOLGRIM
algorithm with the restriction that

u
is a linear combination of at most
n_{0}
of the
functions in
{\cal F}
. We follow the approach proposed in
Remark
10.10
.

For this purpose we fix
\theta\geq 0
and define

\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(n_{0},d,\gamma)>0
and

\beta_{0}=\beta_{0}(n_{0},C,d,\gamma,\theta)>0
by

\lambda_{0}:=\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{n_{0}-1}{D}\omega_{d}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}-1\right)^{-1}>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{0}:=2d^{k}\left(2C\lambda_{0}^{\gamma-k}+\theta e^{\lambda_{0}}\right)>0.

(10.52)

With the value of
\beta_{0}>0
fixed, define a positive
constant
\delta=\delta(n_{0},C,d,\gamma,\theta,q)>0
and an integer

\Lambda=\Lambda(n_{0},C,d,\gamma,\theta,q)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
by

\delta:=\sup\left\{t>0~:~2Ct^{\gamma-q}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}e^{t}\leq\beta_{0}\right\}>0\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda:=\max\left\{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}~:~m\leq\frac{2^{d}}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{d}\right\}.

(10.53)

A consequence of
(
10.38
)
and

(
10.53
)

is that
N_{\cov}([0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\delta)\leq\Lambda
, and so
we may choose a subset

\Sigma=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{\Lambda}\}\subset[0,1]^{d}

for which
[0,1]^{d}\subset\cup_{s=1}^{\Lambda}{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}^{d}(p_{s},\delta)
.
Moreover,

(
10.52
)

ensures that

\frac{2^{d}}{\omega_{d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{d}\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{D}

(10.54)

so that, via
(
10.38
)
, we have that

N_{\pack}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\lambda_{0})\leq\frac{n_{0}-1}{D}
.
Furthermore, it follows from

(
10.52
)

that
0\leq\theta<\beta_{0}/2d^{k}
.

Therefore we may apply the
HOLGRIM
algorithm to
approximate
\varphi
on
\Sigma
with

,

\beta_{0}/2
as the target accuracy in

(A)
,

as the acceptable recombination error in

HOLGRIM

(A)
,

as
the order level in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

s_{1}=\ldots=s_{M}=1

the shuffle numbers in
HOLGRIM

(A)
,

k_{1}=\ldots=k_{M}=1
as the integers

k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
in

HOLGRIM

(A)
, and

A_{1}:=||f_{1}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)},\ldots,A_{{\cal N}}:=||f_{{\cal N}}||_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,\Omega,W)}
as the
scaling factors chosen in
HOLGRIM

.

Observe that

defined in

(

)

coincides with the positive constant

in the

for the constants

, and

as

,
\gamma

\beta_{0}/2
,

and

,

for which the

(10.55)

and such that

\text{for every }l\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\max_{x\in\Sigma}\left\{\Lambda^{k}_{\varphi-u}(x)\right\}\leq\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}.

(10.56)

The condition

.
Recall that the finite subset

Fix

, and

here respectively.

for which the
HOLGRIM

,

(cf.

)
)

\sum_{s=1}^{Q_{m}}\left|c_{s}\right|\left|\left|f_{e(s)}\right|\right|_{{\mathrm{Lip}}(\gamma,[0,1]^{d},{\mathbb{R}})}=C,

(10.62)

(10.63)

)
